Agent interviewed the confidential informant from whom
the information set out in reference repart was obtained for fur-
ther information concerning matters in refarence repert which in-
formant might have. Informant stated that he had very little
specific information other than what is set out in reference
report. However, he indieated that from his contact with the
AMTORO TRADING CORPCRATION (as mentioned in reference repart) and
also from his recent contact with the OKUHA C. MPANY (which asso-
ciation was terminated January 31, 1940) as well as from his
present aseociation w1 th the . . Ki.LlOuG CoMPANY at 225 Broad-
way, he has picked up general information to the effeet that
Japan, Germny, Italy, and Russia are adopting subterfuges for
the purpese of obtaining high octane avistion gas and also fer the
purpese of obtaining blueprints and plans for the construetien of
oil-eracking plants to be used in the production of aviation gas
in those countries.
Informant stated that in 1939 ons H. .  Ll came to the
United  tates from I taly and was in touch with various companies,
uh  M. . KELLOOG C PANY nd UNIV RSA! CII PRODUCTS nd ub-
sequently went back to Italy. it is informant's impressien that
BELl, through his contacts in this country, obtained confidential
information regarding oil-cracking presesses which have been de-
veloped here. Informant indicated that a commission of Germans
came to the United states in the summer of 1939 for the purpese
of obtaining information on oil-cracking processes and that this
commission was in close contact with the . . Ai LLooG CUuPANY.
The following persons were associated with the commission: one
KEINKk, one KAUPMAN, one RIL, one BRIGi, one NH, and one
BELLiR. Informant aid that he understands that KEINKE, KAUrMAN,
and FRIiL returned to jermany on the Bremen in the late summer of
1939 and that Dx101 and viN  went back to Jermany by way of
Japan and ussia after the outbreak of the present world war.
Informant says that he believes that  L may still be in the
United States making contacts with industrial companies here
which are in the business of constructing oil-cracking plants.
Informant indicated that one INiiI oN is chief of the
Catalytic Hydroforming Division of the .. K. Li C PaNy and
that this division is the one which is engaged in research work
on the production of high-grade aviation gass. PAUL T. JRAF is
an assistant or FINLAYSUN. Informant believes that FINLAYSoN and
GRAFF went to Jermany sometime in the past year or year and a
half to consult with German technical experts on various catalytic
processes. In any event, informant is quite certain that FINLAYSON

Informant maid that he understands that the UNIViRSAL

As was indicated in reference report, informant has
stated that Japan utilises a number of Japanese companies in the
United States for the purpose of negotiating deals whereby Japan
may obtain the most modern oil-cracking processes. The OKURA
CuMPANY is one of several Japanese companies which according to
informant are used for this purpose. They hold themselves out
as having no connection with one another but informant states that
they are actually simply agents of the Japanese Navy. He said
that be believes they ure makini use of various subterfuges to
ciroumvent the State epartment's embargo. He said that in one
instance one of these Japanese concerns, the name of which he
does not recall, was in contact with the . . lo C.NFANY
in order to obtain plans for what was alleged to be a dehydrating
plant. Such a dohydratin: plant woul not come within the re-
strictions of the State epartment's moral enbargo.
The ..  .AY furnished the Jpanose 
thorities plane for such a dehydratin, plant. informant says
that actually trese plans upon beins exemined by him were found
to be identical with plans which would be used for construction
of a plant which would produce aviation gas. Informant said that
he had taken this matter up with the officials of the M. A. A iG
Com! Any and that these officials had said that they were goiny to
discuss thie matter with the proper officials of the tate e-
partment.
Informant turned over to reporting agent the plans of
the alleged dehydrating plant, and these plans have been photo-
statted by the New York Jffice. Three photostatic copies of the
plans are being transmitted herewith to the sureau with their
copies of this report, and two copies are being retained in the
New York Jffice files.

supply Japan, though they may have sources of synthetic cryolite.
894.24
Inquiries have come to us from Japan, and we
made one shipment, but hesitate to continue furrishing cryolite
if the resulting aluminum is ant to be used in building airplanes
and other war machinery unfriendly to the United States and other
democratic countries.
If the State Department wishes to exprese an
opinion on this point we shall be glad indeed to receive it as
it is our desire to co-operate, even though there may be no
legal reason for our so doing.

that we require and request from you under the memorandum
agreements between us dated August 19, 1938, designs,
drawings, plans, and material specirications for the
construction and operation of certain catalytic crack-
ing, dehydrogenation, selective catalytic polymerization,
and low pressure hydrogenation plants for which we have
negotiated under the rights obtained from you under the
aforesaid agreements to license such processes with
Chosen Oil Company, Toyo Shoko Oil Company, Japanese
Govermment, Mitsui Mining Company, Mitsubishi Oil Com-
pany, and Nippon Oil Company for installation in Japan
and Korea.
For further negotiations in hand we will also
require, in the near future, similar data and inform-
ation as well as those for the installation of isomer-
ization and alkylation plants and other processes, as
provided in the aforesaid agreements. In addition, we
will from time to time require and request further data
and services pursuant to said agreements.
Will you kindly inform us how soon we may
expect you to dispatoh the above, for which we will
be glad to furnish any further information that you
may require in the preparations theseof?
The foregoing is, of course, without Ire-
judice to our rights under the aforesaid agreements,
our rights to place with you further orders that we
are now nogotiating and may in the future nogotiate
with licensees, and to our rights in the cases pend-
ing between us.
Yours sincerely,
JAPAN GASOLINE COMPANY, LTD.

August 23, 1940, adaressed by Mr. H. J. Tim-
perley, London, to Mr. Harry B. Price, Executive
Beoretary of the American Committee for Non-
Participation in Japanese Aggression, New York,
in regard to the Granby Copper Mine in Canada.
Mr. Adams discussed the matter with
Kr. Wailes of EU. Mr. Wailes telephoned to
someone in the Canadian Legation and asked in-
formally whether the Canadian Legation had any
information in regard to reported sales of copper
by the Granby Copper Mine to Japan. Mr. Wailes
was told that the Legation did have some informa-
tion about the Granby Copper Mine but did not
know anything specific about the reported three-
year contract. Mr. Wailes was told that the
activities of the mine had been tne subject of
some questions in the Canadian Parliament. The
Canadian Legation is aware that the mine is
selling copper to Japan, the Granby Copper Mine
claiming that the grade of copper which it manu-
factures has no other market than Japan.
Mr. Wailes indicated that it would be pos-
sible, if FE decided that such action was de-
sirable, for him to write a semi-personal letter
to someone in the American Legation in Canada
and ask that he get in touch with the Counselor
of the Canadian Ministry of External Affairs
who

=== Page 61 === (confidence 0.94)

DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-2-
who had or could obtain full information con-
cerning the Granby Copper Mine.
In as much as the United States, during
1939, sold about $22,000,000 worth of copper
to Japan as against some $3,000,000 worth of
copper from Canada, and in as much as copper
is not on our own restricted list, it is sug-
gested that no useful purpose would be served
by endeavoring to obtain precise information
concerning the activities of the Granby Copper
Mine.

ANGLO-IRANIAN OIL COMPANY (SALES, JAPAN)
Mr. Mander asked the Under-Secretary of State for Foreign
affairs whether, in view of the closin of the Burma Road for the
upply of war materials to Chira, it is proposed to cancel the sale
of 1 000,000 barrels of oil to Japan made in ril, 1?40, by the
British Government-controlled Anglo-Iranian Cil Company with the
express approval of the British Governmont?
Mr. Butler: I have nothing further to add to the reply given
by my hon. Friend the  inister of .etroleum or this subject on 23rd
Apri1.
Mr. Lander: Could anythiny be nore crossly unfair than to
supply Japan with oil to attack China at the same time as China is
refused permission to receive supplics along the Burma hoad?
Mr. Butler: His Majesty's Goveruaent on te ate mentioned
said that it was not thoir policy to intorfere with the commercial
activities of this country.
Mr. Stokes: o wat cxtent are His majosty's Governmont being
influenced by vested intorests in oil?
Mr. Noel-Baker: Is it not ost desirable that the Tovernment
should avoid the charge of vin, in effect, put an oil sanction
upon a victim of ag resaion whilo continuing to supply the aggressor
with most of his requiremonts?
Mr. Butler: I agroc that there is foree in the hon. Member's
point, but the Government has always endeavoured to keep themselves
free from such charges. I  ave rcferrcd to the original answer
given by the Minister of  etroleum. The hon. eber will see from
that answer that there is no other li it to this transaction.
Mr. icGovern: Is this policy supportod by the Labour members
of the Governmont?
r. Butler: All Jovernnent ecisions are unanimous.
Mr. Noel-Baker: Will the right hon. Gentleman undertake to
have this matter reconsidered, in viow of the fact that it is not
possible to lay down a principle like that enunciated in the reply
referred to in April, and that tho Governnent must consider the
political and other bearings of comercial transactions in petroleum?
Mr. Butler: I am certainly willing to communicate the views
expressed by the hon. Member to my hon. Friend the inister for
Petroleum.

Hon. Summer Wells, Under Sec. of State,

894.24/11
Dear Sir;— I am glad to see that you are embargoing shipments of
oil, etc. to Japan.
Your caurse in tnis respect is definitely right. We will gain no
friendship from Japan by supplying her with oil, scrap iron, etc. but
she will not hesitate to use them against us whenever she thinks best to do
so. By supplying her with war materials we are strengthening her when she
chooses to tunan against us. nd she will turn against us just as soon
whether we do or do not. She is like the rest of the totalitarians, has
no conscience. I think we make a great mistake in being soft towerd any
of them.
Another thing, I am uncertain about sup lying the South American coun-
tries with what may be used against us. Those countries, including Mexico,
are not in fact friendly to us, or if so, onèy so long as they can get some-
thing out of us and would not hesitate one minute to turn against us and use
against us what we had supplied them. They are oracticully totalitarians
now and mostly German, Italian and Spanish, all of which nationalitbes h te us.
My 78 years of experiance and study have taught(me something.
Very Sincerely,
I think we ought to support Brita n to the limit whether we ever get
nay or not for what we furnish. She is fighting bur battle and if we can
at comparatively small expense help ner to win, we will not have to eventually
assume the whole urden of attack.

=== Page 77 === (confidence 0.99)
THE FAR EAST. A PROPOSAL FOR ACTION.
1. THE SITUATION
Germany dominates the continent of Europe, possesses
a military machine unrivalled in the history of the world,
and has given the clearest indications of an intention to
use that machine for the purpose of becoming dominant through-
out the world. Jepan, nourished by imports of war materials
from the United States and sustained by the proceeds of ex-
ports to the United States, is on the point of establishing
horeelf as the dominant power in the Far East and is devoting
all her energies to thot purpose. Japan has Joined forces
with Germany and Italy with a view of pushing military con-
quest to its uttermost limits and extending economic domina-
tion to those regions where military conquest may be impracti-
cable. There are two major obstacles which Japan must over-
come before she can pursue her designs and become an effective
partner of the Axis powers. (1) She must win the Chincse
war, where her energies are being dissipated in the con-
quest of 400,000,000 people, cager and able to fight but
lacking the supplics and munitions necessary to carry on much
longer an effective war. (2) She must acquire undisputed
naval dominance in the South Pacific.
2. THE THREAT TO THE UNITED STATES
If Japan is successful in overcoming these obstacles,
the course of events is marked with substantial certcinty:
n. Japan would seize and absorb Indo-China, Sirm and
the Dutch East Indies.
b.
With these possessions consolidated in Japanese hands,
there would be no further offective obstacle to the con-
quest of all British possessions in the Fa East, includ-
ing Australia.
C.
The most that the British could hope for as a result
of the present war would be the successful defense of
their Island against German invasion---a defense which
would be rendered even more prec rious by the fact thet
Germany had acquired the Japanese alliance.
d.
With the Axis powers dominating Europe and Japan
dominating the East, cconomic control of South America
by these three would be a probability; and history has
shown that economic domination merges by imperceptible
degrees into political domination.
In a world where the foregoing events had taken place
the position of the United States would not be an enviable
one. In the firet plrce, there would be a constant neces-
sity of maintcining a military and naval establishment of
wartime proportions, with the attend nt disruption of our
pencetime pursuits, dissipation of our national income in
armaments nd waste of millions of valuable yenrs of the
lives of our young men in militery service. Second, our
foreign trnde would be substantially eliminated, a condi-
tion which could not fail to ffect, directly or indirectly,

=== Page 78 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
every person living in this country. Third, the enforce-
ment of the Monroe Doctrine would become an impossibility
due to the extent of the pressure that could be exerted
upon South America and the extent of our preoccupation
with our own defense. And is it too imaginative to suggest
that ultimately we should have to fight?
3. WHAT WE CAN DO NOW
The conditions precedent to the success of Japan are
the conquest of China and the acquisition of naval dominance
in the South Pacific. We can prevent both of these condi-
tions from being fulfilled; but this can be done only by
immediately and vigorously taking the initiative. The steps
that must be taken to accomplish this result are the follow-
ing:
a.
We must deprive Japan of raw materials which she
must have to wage effective war. The embargo on avia-
tion gasoline and scrap are steps in the right direc-
tion. They should be followed by embargoes on pig
iron, copper, oil, gasoline, motor vehicles and mach-
inery, all of which can now be freely shipped to Japan.
Moreover, there should be an embargo on imports from
Japan, for without exporting to this country, Japan can-
not acquire the credits necessary to the acquisition of
materials she must buy.
b.
We should give all possible aid to China. This means
granting substantially unlimited credits to the Chiang
Kai-shek government. In meeting the Japanese and Axis
menace, it is far better that we provide, gratis if nec-
essary, the munitions to enable the Chinese to fight
than that, sooner or later, we sacrifice not only our
munitions but also the lives of our young men. The
Burma Road is available to transport all supplies the
Chinese could purchase. English cooperation in re-open-
ing this road is a foregone conclusion since Japan joined
the Axis.
C.
We should arrange with Great Britain to share or
assume control of the Singapore Naval Base and other
British bases in the East, so far as this is practicable
from the point of view of sound naval stretegy. The
United States navy is now superior to that of Japan.
This condition may not long continue, for it is probable
that there are Japanese ships under construction adequate
in numbers, tonnage and modernity to tip the balance
against us. With the United States navy based at Singa-
pore, Japanese conquest of the East Indics from distant
bases would be a practicel impossibility.

=== Page 79 === (confidence 0.86)
-3-
4. WOULD THE COUNTRY BACK THIS ACTICN?
Up to the present time the national adninistration
could not be blamed for its inactivity, for the Ancrican
people have believed in the protective power or the sens
that surround us and have hnd a rather vague conviction
that somehow or other the kind of wickedness we hove been
observing would not long prevoil and that, ns before, the
British would prove invincible. But a changc has taken
place. The country is now awoke. Since the conquert of
Norway nnd the Low Countrics vwe have leorned the lesson
which Munich and the conqucet of Czcchoelavokin could not
tench us: that to permit the preantory notions to take and
rold the initintivi lends to disrster; that a belief in the
power of appensument is a life in a fool's poradisc; and that
the only method of denline wit tis totelitorion powers is
to net before they net and thwart them before the? hove nou-
risied thenselvrs b. conguert into a coneition of irresict-
ible strength.

Dear Mr. Welles:

been aghast at the unreadiness of the administration to act
promptly in the embargo of essential war materials to Japan and
prohibition of the importation of silk. To the Oriental, our
dilatory tactics mean only one thing - that we are afraid. This
is the best policy for the promotion of war.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
Dear Mr. melles:
This is a somewhat belated line to congratulate you upon
the excellent presentation of our Far Eastern policy which you de-
livered at Cleveland. Naturally, looking back one can easily seo
certain sins of both commission and omission ( particularly, I be-
lieve, of omission ); but I thoroughly agree with you our policy was
sound and well handled.
Further, I do think it clear that from now on the posi-
tive aspects of the policy deserve emphasis. There is obviously
nothing to be gained by half measures or by soft peddling the issues.
I would like to urge upon you the desirability of extending the Embargo
to copper, petroleum products, pi-iron, and all steel products.
These all are indispensable sinews of war and should not be allowed
894.24
to reach any of the Axis powers or their allies. Such an Embargo
would be a fitting answer to the threat implicit in the alliance,
and should serve, if anything can, to make clear to the military
leaders of Japan just how effective their alliance with Russia and
Italy is. of course, such an embargo should be taken into conjuno-
tion with England, Canada, Australia, and Dutch East Indies; otherwise,
the Embargo will not be completely effective.
I trust, too, that some early opportunity will be taken
to make available to American naval forces the use of the Singapore
811.34544
base and that in exchange for this facility the so-called Flying
Fortreeses and our bomb sight be made available to Great Britain.
I am sending copies of this letter to Senators Sheppard
and Connally and Congressman Mansfield in the hopes that the expres-
sion of any such policy outlined above by the Administration will re-
ceive their wholehearted support.
pempw
Yours very truly,
36
Hon. Morris Sheppard
Hon. Tom Connally
Hon. J. J. Mansfield

hibiting the sale of the Japanese rights to such
process to the Japanese or other interested parties
for exploitation in Japan?
The process in question is a method of
2
removing the oil content from oil-bearing shale.
5
894.24
This process is in no way a refining process. It is
merely a method of removing oil from oil-bearing
shale.
Is there any place in Los Angeles or San
Francisco where we may keep up to date on regulations
in this connection?
Before writing you I was in touch with the
customs house in San Francisco and they were unable
to give me any information.
Yours very truly,
R.m. Wurray
R. M. MURRAY

=== Page 118 === (confidence 0.99)
EISENBERG'S
LEXINGTON & CENTRAL AVE.
BALTIMORE, MD.
October 2, 1940
The Honorable Lansdale Sasscer
House of Representatives
Washington, D. C.
Dear Sir:
As your constituent I wish to express my views and
desires in a very recent event.
England 1s today paying the price for not having
listened to Churchill and many other British statesmen,
when they were warning England that the Nazis are preparing
for another world war. For the past six years they kept
drumming this same information to the British politicians,
but with no avail, and up until just a few weeks before the
declaration of war, England was selling war material to
Germany.
In all probability, British big business was more
interested in the financial gain than they were in the
fact that they might pay back this profit one hundred fold,
and then some, in a possible war with Germany. Which I
think was a very bad gamble.
We have the same thing going on now here in our
United States. We are selling today to the Japanese raw
materials that in all probability will be used against us.
The president has put an embargo on scrap iron, this em-
bargo not to take effect until October l6th, I can not see
why this embargo should not be put into immediate exeoution.
Also petroleum, pig iron, gin cotton, and suoh other
supplies that go into the making of war, these thinge also
should be put on the 1mmediate embargo l1st to Japan. The
few paltry dollars that our exporters can make in selling
this material to Japan, can not, and will not, even com-
prise a millionth pary of what a Japanese-American war
would cost us in lives and property, if we keep Japan
supplied with the materials that make for war, and has
given Japan the strength and power that she now has.
I wish that you coulc visualize the seriousness of
this situation and use your good offices to affect such an
1mmediate embargo.
Very truly yours
EISENBERG'S
By: B. N. EISENBERG

hepartmont of
Participants:
Mr. Welles
Mr. Cornelius F. Kelley, Chairman of the
Board, Anaconda Copper Company
Dr. Feis
Mr. J. R. Hobbins, President, Anaconda
Copper Compapy
Mr. Daniels (RA) Mr. C. T. Ulrich, Vice President
Kennecott Copper Corporation, 120 Broadway,
New York.
894.24/11
Mr. Kelley outlined the s1tuation relating to copper
shipments from Chile to Japan as follows:
During recent months copper exports from Chile to
Japan have shown an extraordinary increase, more than
894.24
2
double normal requirements. In view of the ourtailment
6
of copper exports from the United States to Japan, the
Japanese have been requesting that the copper companies
fill their orders with Chilean copper. The companies
realize the gravity of the situation, and did not weloome
the 1dea of being placed in the position of selling to
Japan a raw material which might be turned against this
country in the event of hostilities. At the same time,
F/FG
if the companies refused to sell Chilean copper to Japan,
they would in effect be declaring an embargo on such
shipments.

=== Page 130 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
shipments. To do so would, they felt, create diplomatic
complications between the Governments of Japan and the
United States, as well as between Japan and Chile; and
in addition would expose the companies to severe criticism
in Chile and possibly affeot relations between the Govern-
ments of Chile and the United States. In the latter con-
nection figures were presented indicating the extent to
which Chile depended on dollar revemues derived from
copper exports, and it was demonstrated that a sharp
curtailment in this industry would have serious economic
and political repercussions in that country. Mr. Hobbins
also stated that his company had just received reports to
the effect that serious labor troubles were threatening
to occur in northern Chile in the large nitrate and copper
plants.
Mr. Welles expressed full understanding of the diffi-
culty of the problem, adding that he could not well
underestimate the gravity of the Far Eastern situation.
He suggested that, if the companies were to sign any
further contracts for delivery of Chilean copper to the
Japanese, these contracts be for the shortest possible
term and contain a clause providing that, if the interests
of hemispheric defense require, the Governments of the
United States and Chile would be given prior lien over
such copper.
Mr. Kelley

=== Page 131 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
Mr. Kelley then referred to the production of copper
in the United States, stating that at the present rate of
consumption stocks were being depleted and, in the absence
of imports, the United States would face a deficiency by
next year. He stated that he was presenting this aspeot
of the matter to the National Defense Commission this
afternoon, in order that the Commission might be in a
position to take such action as the circumstances warranted.
Dr. Feis raised the question as to whether the existing
excise tax would apply to copper imoorted by the Metals
Reserve Corporation for defense purposes and it was agreed
that this point should be clarified. Mr. Kelley stated
that Chilean copper production was more than ample to
satisfy the needs of the United States, annual production
running between four and five hundred thousand tons.
Mr. Welles agreed that it would be desirable for Mr. Kelley
and the others to present the facts in this matter to the
National Defense Commission, which would be in the best
position to make recommendations and take action.
With respect to the proposed clause to be included
in subsequent contracts of the companies, it was agreed
that a draft would be sent to Dr. Feis for consideration
by the Department prior to including it in any new contracts.
Statistical tables attached hereto giving detailed
information on various of the points brought out above
were

MENORANDUM
There is submitted herewith three statements:
1. Financial statement covering the dollars or their
•quivalent paid by the Chile Exploration Company and Andes
Copper Mining Company, subsidiaries of Anaconda Copper Min-
ing Company, to the Republic of Chile for the period Jan-
uary 1, 1939 to September 30, 1940. The purpose of this
statoment 1s to show the important contributions made by
the c opper companies to the national economy of Chile.
2. Statement which shows how through the afective-
ness of the British blockade the markets to which Chilean
copper flowed from January 1, 1939 to September 30, 1940,
wore gradually eliminated.
3. Statement which shows the total sales and commit-
ments of subsidiaries of the Anaconda Copper Mining Company
to deliver coppor at New York or Chile for Japanese account,
the latter statement being the position as of October 11,
1940, all enumerated contracts to be filled from Chilean
production.

MEMORANDUM
Re- Copper Position
-000-
A conference was held in Mr. Kelley's office Wednesday,
Ootober 9th, at which were present Messrs. Kelley, Hobbins, Dwyer,
Sowerwine and Kennedy. The general copper situation was discussed.
This memorandum summarizes the subject matter of that discussion
based upon figures now available. The periods covered are 1- The
four months period ending December 31, 1940, and 2 - The six months
period ending June 30, 1941.
1 - FOUR MONTHS PERIOD ENDING DECEMBER 31, 1940
Domestic Copper Supply -
The domestic copper stock that will be available at the
close of the year is estimated by Mr. Eckert as follows:

stock of 50,000 tons, which seems low, as we estimate that under

=== Page 150 === (confidence 0.97)

-2-
present conditions 75,Oo0 tons would be the low figure. However,
Mr. Eokert's figure mey hold as undoubtedly some of the refineries
will carry a part of their working stocks in foreign copper.
The foregoing figures indicate a consumption of l00,308
tons per month, which undoubtedly includes a certain tonnage of
domestic copper that will be fabricated for export. Allowing
5,oo0 tons for this purpose, the indicated domestic consumption is
about 95,000 tons per month. Approximately the same domestic con-
sumption is arrived at by computation from the Brass and Wire and
Cable Companies' requirements for the last quarter.

year is estimated at 95,000 tons per month. From indioations as to
outside business, consumption during the first half of 194l should
increase at least 10% to say 105,000 tons. The Brass preliminary
estimates for the first quarter of 194l show an increase in domestic
of about 40% and the Wire and Cable of 20% to 25% over August.

is probably low under present operating conditicns. If domestic
consumption does not show an increase over the estimated consump-
tion for the last quarter of this year of 95,000 tons, the figures
for the first half would show a break-even.
The foregoing is on the assumption that stocks are evenly
distributed, which of course is not the case, and shortages will
undoubtedly develop in certein quarters towerd the close of the
year if present rate of consumption msintuins. With consumption
of 105,000 tons per monthn and procuction of 88,000 tons, it is
evident that the domestic copper merket will require 8 supply of
about 17,ooo tons per month fron foreign sources beginning soon
after the first of the year. In addition it will also require from
foreign sources whatever copper is used by domestic fabricators for
export.

On the basis of the foregoing setup, in which we assume
that our fabricating plants will do 30% of the business done by all
fabricators, the consumptive demand through our plants and outside
souroes would total 174,000 tons, or 29,000 tons per month, as compared
with total domestic production of 18,000 tons per month, a deficit of
11,Ooo tons per month. For all others the consumption is estimated
at 456,000 tons, or 76,000 tons per month, as compared with a produc-
tion of 70,000 tons per month, or a deficit of 6,000 tons per month.
On the assumption that fabrication for export will not dry
up entirely it is reasonable to assume from present indications that
the domestic fabricator will require foreign copper to the extent
of 20,000 to 25,000 tons per month.
The foreign copper situation, including Anaconda's position,
will be dealt with in a separate memorandum.

5

China.
nut

=== Page 173 === (confidence 0.99)
COP
Ootober 19, 1940
President Franklin D. Roosevelt
The White House
Washington, D. C.
Dear Sir:
I write to say that I have been following closely
and with keen interest your policles with regard to the
1mcreasing aggresslons of Japan in the Far Mast.
Please let me congratulate you on the action taken
so far in outtimg off American exports of aviation gasolime
and sorap steel. These measures should in my very earmest
opinion be followed as soon as possible by embargoes on
copper, pig iron, motor vehicles, etc., these being now, as
I understand, freely shipped to Japan every day. And I
would go even farther and wisn that we could embargo important
Japanese imports to this country, because an mbargo of that
sort would make it difficult or impossible for Japan as a
matter of exchange to purchase essential war materials in
this country.
Moreover, I think that the use of the Singapore base
by our Navy or units thereof should be seriously considered
by the administration at ashington. If Japanese aggressiom
1s allowed to go on at its present rate, we may be involved
later in a struggle with her which would be far more cestly
in treasure and men than any preventive steps we might take
during the next few weeks or months.
Before closing, I wish to congratulate you also on
your measures to aid Great Britain. These measures are
certainly sound as national defense and as aids to the con-
sorvation of British civilization.
You have many warm supporters on this campus, many
of them being your champions on account of your foreim
policies, and I think you will receive a good many votes on
November 5.
Sincerely and faithfully yours,
Bessie C. Randolph
BCRILD
President
P. S. I am sending a copy of this letter to Secretary Ball
my colleague in the American Society of International Law.

ESTABLISHED 1883
DULUTH,MINNESOTA
November 16, 1940
Honorable Henry Morgenthau
Secretary of the Treasury
Washington, D. C.
Sir:
In connection with the activities for the efficient
defence of the United States, should not the questians-as
outlined below be given serious consideration and such action
be taken as will speed up and improve the present defensive
action?
1. Is it not true that Japan is still purchasing without
restriction large quantities of basic war materials from the
United States, and that these purchases are still financed
through the unrestricted sale of Japanese goods in the American
market?
2. If the great majority of the Am rican people wish to
see the present embargoes on war supplies extended, should
not further action be taken?
3. If Japan has joined the Axis powers in an alliance
against the United States, should we not stop furnishing to
Japan war materials for possible future use against us?
4. Present aid to china is still very small. houlc not
such further aid to china be given at this time; such aid as
will assist Britain and will act as a check on Japan?
Respectfully yours,
Am prinale

On the New York Herald Tribune Forum

Sst iweek ago three important events occurred almost within
い
twerg-four hours. : On October l6th the American embargo on all
of,sorap iron and steel became effective. On the same day.
17.0,000 Americaln youths between the ages of 21 and 35, inclusive, 
regi tered under the Selective Service Ast and by exactly midnight of
the same day the famous Burma Road, after three months of closing, was
reopened to the transportstior of Chinese war materials.
Probably it wes mere coircidence thst these three everts occured
on the same day. But the fsct that they did ocour almost simultaneously
44
should make us better understard their significance. "hat is the
signifioenoe? It is that the Anglo-Soxon world now fully recognizes
and understands that China and the British Empire are fighting on the
same side in one and the samo world war which is threatoning to funda-
montally affeot, if not to militarily involve, the American Republic.
893.24
That is why Great Britain is opening tho Burma Road to Chinose
traffio in flat defiance of Japanese threats. That is why the
793.94
Amerioan Government is at long last placing all grades of scrap iron
and steel under embargo. And that is why the United States for the
PS/D
first time is drafting her millions of youths for military training
in peace time.
Almost four months ago, speaking at the New York World's Fair on
July 5th, I made this statement:
"It is very important to realize - to understand-
that there are today two great powers, ard only two, that
00
are by force of arme desperately resisting aggression. One
is the British Empire. The other is China. And China
has been at it for three long, hard and, defensively, suooessful 
years. Fighting our own battle, we havdoalso helped those
who are fighting the same sort of battle elsewhere: we have
rondered one of the most formidable of the aggressors in-
capable of going to the aid of its European prototypes and
partnere

=== Page 211 === (confidence 0.98)
8.
- 2 -
pertners in aggression."
At that time those words were not fully appreoiated by many.
Certainly the British Government which two weeks later olosed the
Burme Road to China, did not fully realize that she was strangling
an ally and helping her enemies. And there were not a few publicists
who thonght and openly advocated that Japan should be appeased so that
both Great Britain and the United States might concentrate their
effort ir dealing with the European situation.
Then towards the end of last month came the announcement of the
signing of the Triple Alliance by the terms of whioh Japan recognizes
and respects the leadership of Germany and Italy in Europe, while
Germany and Italy recogr.ize and resnect the leadership of Japan in the
so-oalled "Greater East Asia". Under Artiole 3 of the same treaty,
these three aggressor-states "further ur.dertake to assist one another
with all political, economic and military means when one of the three
contracting powers is attacked by a power st present not involved in
the European War or in the Chineso-Japanese Conflict". In a speech
following the signatures, Herr von Ribbertrop frarkly stated:
"Any state, should it harbor the intentior of mixing
in the final phase of the solution of these probleme in
Europe or Eastern Asia or attacking one state signatory
to this three-power pact, will have to teke on the entire
concentrated might of three nations with more than 250,000,000
inhabitants".
And a few days later Foreign Minister Yosuke Matsuo ka openly warned
the United States:
"If America is going to blindly and stubbornly stiok
to the status quo in the Pacific, then we will fight America".
This new Triple Alliance and the threatening words that came
after it have offered to the whole world the corolusive evidenoe that
Japan has been fighting on the side of Germany and Italy. Indeed
Japan has been their ally ever since the signing of the Tripartite
Anti-Comintern Paot of November 19?6. Japar's inability to render
igreater sssistnnoe to her Europeen allies during these months of the
European War

=== Page 212 === (confidence 0.97)
-8 .
Fopean War, - that oan only be explained by the fact that she hee
been too deeply bogged down in her three-year war in China. She has
lost a million men; she hes grently depleted her long acoumuleted
stook of arms and lethal weapons; her commerce, her industries for
xport, and her national finance have all been virtually ruined by the
war: her gold holdings are almost completely exhausted; and her navy.
seoond only to the navies of Great Britain and the United States, has
been needed to keep the supply lines open to her China war front whieh
now exterds over 2000 miles.
In short, it is Chira's three years fighting which has so greatly
weakened and disabled Jepen that she is no longer free nor fully
capable either to effestively heln her European allies, or to take ad-
vartage of the uronuan War to loot ard plunder whenever she finds it
most profitable.
This, I think, is the historiorl significanoe of China's part in
tho present world struggle to save demooracy.
Our general problem, however, is "Saving Democracy". Democracy
has always beel. erdangered by powerful militeristic invaders. It is
no mere historical aocidert that the developmert of modern democratio
institutions took place first in: the British Isles ard later in North
America. Isolation from militaristic invasion was then a neoessary
condition for the gradual develonment of free democratic institutions.
But the world is now made so small that isolation is no longer
possible; and that the problem of "3aving Demooraoy" has become one
indivisible problem of saving democracy for the world in general. A
world whioh is unssfe for the life ard liberty of my people, cannot
long be safe for the Amerioan ways of life. A world in which such
truly oivilized, democratio and peace-lovirg peoples as the Danes, the
Norwegians. the Dutoh nd the Belgians find no safety nor seourity.
oennot possibly be safe for suoh great powers as France, Great Britain
and the United States of America. The Amerioan Republio is now
oonegipting her youths in peace time, and has plarned to spend 50
b1111on

=== Page 213 === (confidence 0.97)
- 4 -
to eollars on her natioral defense, simply because this world is
fe for domooraoy, here ns elsowhere.
Therefore, the problem of "Saving Demooraoy" is the s me old
problem of "making the world sefe for democracy".
wenty-throe years ago, tho Amorioan nation took up the fight to
"mmke the world safu for domocracy". You won the war, but lost the
peaco. You chose to shirk the gruat task of building up a new world
ordur whioh was to enforce peaco and to ond all wars.
The result was a world order with no power to enforcc its own
duoisions and laws. You and I have witnessod that world ordor being
ruthlessly assaulted ard completely scrapped.
And we are here again assembled to think over the same old prob-
lem, "Saving Demoorecy".
The key to the solutior. of this problem is still in the hands of
the American people. Keep out of the wor if you can. But two things
you must do. First, you must give every effective aid to China and the
British Empire to win the war. And, secondly, when the war is won, as
I am sure it will be wor, you must . ectively participate in making and
maintaining the peace so that there may be built up a new international
struoture with great organized power behind it to meke the world safe
for democrasy safe for humanity to live in. Without America, the
task will surely fail again.

CHINA FIGHTS 
An address
by
His Exoellenoy Dr. Hu Shih
The Chinese Ambassador to the United States
before
The Merohants' Association of New York
at the Hotel Astor, New York City
Thursday, O0tober 24, 1940, 12:15 PM
By the erd of the first week of November, China's war of resis-
tanoe to Japanese aggression will re forty months old. China has
been fighting for three years and four months.
I nsed not tell this en?ightened assembly how terribly hard this
war hes been for an uuprepared and peace-loving people like my people.
You know well that China has lost vast and important territories and
has suffered stuperdous casuelties. You know well that our enemy has
been and is still in  lmo:t complete cortrol of the air,the sea end
the importart lines o' railweys and water-ways. You krow well that
practioally all the cities known to you as centers of trade end menu-
facture, of education and culture, - from Harbin to Nanrinz,from
Pe! ing to Canton, from Shanghai to Iohang, - have been either devas-
tate! or occupied by the invaders.
You know well that practioally all the importent universities
and solleges have been in exile in the irterior and are mostly opcrat-
ing without bocks, without laboratory equipment and in frequert
darger of bombirgs from the eir. You know well that for two years
wo havo been practically cut off from sll eccess to the sea snd have
l.d to rely or thrce back doors for getting all our war supplies ficn
icad snd for sending out our exports with which to secure our
fore:gn exohange. And you know well that out of these three back doors,
the overland route from Soviet Russin is only good for the transporta-
tion of goods of light weight; that'the railway route through Fronch
Indo-China hae boor olosed sinoe the collepse of France last summer
and is now praotiorlly seized by the Japenese militnry foroes of
invasion; and that the famoue Burmr Rond was closod by Great Britain
for three months and was re-opened only last week.
I am here today not to tell you in detail these tales of woe and
suffering and almost unsurmountable difficulties. Ne! I am here to
tell you that in spite of these, China still fights on.
You are witnessing two living miraoles; the Battle of Britain
and the

=== Page 219 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
a the Battle of China. And the world is beginning to realise that
Chime and the British Empire are the only two great nations whioh are
ill by force of arms fighting and resisting aggression and that
China has been at it for three long and hard years.
You will ask why and how has China been able to fight so long
under suoh tremendous difficulties wher more powerful and militarily
better prepared nations in Europe have succumbed one after another.
I shall endeavor to give a few explanations.
China has been gifted by nature and ancestral inheritance in
three things which the nations in Europe that have succumbed do not
possess: first, vast space to move about in; second, large numbers
in population as aotual and potential supply of man power; and third,
an historical sense of national unity.
The first two of these, vnst space and large numbers, are so
obvious that no amplification is necessary. I shall only say here
that Japanese military occupation of Chinese territory only covers the
large oities and the lines of transportation and that it is very
doubtful whether to this day Japan can claim effective control over
more than one-tenth of Chinese territory. With regard to numbers,
even the Japanese military high commend has openly admitted that
Generalissimo Chiang Kri-shek still has 3,000,000 trained soldiers
under his command, - that is to sey, even Jspan cdmits that the size
of the Chinese army, not counting the vest guerilla forces, is greeter
today than it was three years ago when the war started.
The third asset, namely, the historical sense of national unity,
needs a few words of amplification. It is not true that China has
been unified by the Japanese invasion and by the three years of war,
for you know suoh a miracle cannot happen within so short a time.
The Chinese national unity has been the product of 2l centuries'
making. China was unified into an empire about 200 B.C. Sinoe then
there have been short periods of separation and of foreign invasion
and domination, but broadly speaking, China has been living almost
cortinously for 2l certuries under one empire, one government, one
law, one written larguage, one system of education and one historical
cuature. This continuity of national life under a urified empire has
no parallel in the history of any other race or continent, and can be
fully

=== Page 220 === (confidence 0.98)
- 3 -
mppreoiated by you only when you try to imagine the Roman
pire as continuing to exist unbroken to this day. The foreign
eserver who writes about Chinese disunity on the basis of the few
years of internal fighting during the early years of the Republio,
has failed to grasp the fundamental sense of national unity which has
always prevailed throughout the ages in spite of politicel strife and
in spite of foreign invasion.
In addition to these three basic national assests, there are other
reasons why China has been able to fight s, long. One of these reasons
1s the oomparatively unorganized economic life of the vast hirterland
of China whioh, as you know, is essertially agrioultural and little
industrialized. Military devastation and aerial bombings are power-
less in crcountering this vast continent of scattered and decentralized
farm villages.
Another reason is the ability of our people to work hard and to
endure great hardships. My neople love life and know how to enjoy it,
but they can also work most heroically in the face of necessity. The
Government has in the last three years transported into the interior
over 250,000 tons of maohinery, so that new factories can be started
in the inland provinoes for the making of arms and other necessities.
This vast amount of maohinery has been carried sometimes in jinriok-
shas, sometimes on river boats and sometimes on human shoulders. And
the heroism with which our people have built the vast network of over-
land motor roads, including the Burma Road and the roade to Russia,
is well attested by all travellers who have gone over these roads and
have seen how our engineers and workers accomplish these engineering
feats with very little modern equipment. It is this oapacity for hard
work whioh has enable our Government to go on with the national cam-
paign of resistanoe to aggression.
Another reason is that our economio reconstruotion in the interior
of China is already showing important results. We are produoing more
food stuff, more ooal, more tungsten, more antimony, more tin, more
gold and more tung oil than ever before. And we have managed to ship
most of our foreign-exohange-producing exports out of the country.
All these naturally help to bolster up our morale and our fighting
ability.
Lastly

=== Page 221 === (confidence 0.99)
-4 -
ly. we have been able to fight on because we have been able
moive some important assistance from our friends abroad. For
wee years China has been receiving aid in one form or another from .
Greet Britain, France, Soviet Russia, and the United States. This
assistanoe has taken various forms, some in the form ef commercial
oredits and loane, some in the form of military supplies purchased
on long-term oredits or under barter, some in the direotion of main-
taining our air routes and trede routes open for our communication wi th
the outside world end trensport-tion of our exports and imports. With
Frenoe collapsed, with Great Britain hard pressed and with Soviet
Rugsia threatened on both western and eastern fronts, our trade routes
and our axternal supolies have been in danger of being closed. But
the re-cpening of the Burma Road and the determination of the American
leaders, both in and outside the Government, to give ample aid to the
netions fighting for their freedom and independence, have led us to
hope that China may yet continue to receive assistanoe from our friends
who are able to render us that assistance. I can only add thet without
the aid from our friundly nations, China could not have fought so well
and so long.
These then are some of the reasons why China has been able to
fight on.
But as I have said at the beginring of my speech, it has been a
terribly hard fight for my people. It is terribly hard to pit human
flesh and blood and human courage against much superior mechanioal and
metal equipment. And, because it is suoh a terribly hard and unequal
fight, kind-hearted friends often ask us: Why can't the war be stopped?
Why go on fighting any longer? Why doesn't Great Britain or the Urited
Stetes come forward to help end the war in the Far East so that the
attention and effort of the Anglo-Saxon world may be concentrated on
the European situation?
These and some other similar questions, I am sure, you have often

monthe sinoe the outbreak of the European War. I shall take the re-

wild not give up the fight.
In the

=== Page 222 === (confidence 0.98)

the firet place, China canuot give up the fight now because
himg up the fight now means complete surrender of our national

md China does not wish to suffer the same fate as rance is now
oufferivg.
Two years ago I made a speech in this great city in whioh I

The founding fathers of the

Army in 1777, we are surely out of Valley Forge elready and we cannot

His Majesty's Chargé d'Affaires has been instructed
ammediately, and before the return of the Ambassador, to in-
vite the U.S. Government to examine the question of oil
imports by Japan.
2.
In view of the unforeseeable potentialities of the Far
Eastern situation, H.M.G. have themselves recently re-examined
the Japanese oil position in the light of estimates used in
the recent discussions in Washington. In these estimates a
stock figure of 5,250,000 tons was arrived at. The correct-
ness of these calculations depends largely upon the accuracy
of the U.S.G.'s estimate of service consumption during the
last three years.
3.
Our Service departments have no estimates for consump-
tion by the Japanese Army, but fron the figures available to
us for consumption by the Japanese Navy and Air Force and
judging fron our estimates of German Army consumption, it
appears to H..G. thatthe estimates used for service consump-
tion of the last three years in arriving at a stock figure
of 5,250,000 tons are too high.
4.
If this is the case it would mean -
(a) That Japanese stocks are somewhat higher than
the figure of 5,250,000 tons.
(b) That, consumption being somewnat lower than
estimated, the current rate of Japanese im-
ports wili result in a considerable further
accumulation of stocks.
5.
In a war in which the Japanese Havy was fully occupied
H.M.G. estimate that total consuaption, even after allowing for
a further reduction in civilian consunption, would be not less
than 7,250,000 tons, and it appears that oii is the most impor
tant Japanese deficiency.
6.
His Majesty's Government are therefore anxious to
examine with the U.3. Government
一
A.
Whether current Japanese imports of oil are running
at such a level as to result in a further accumu-
lation of stocks.
B.
Whether, if (A) is confirmed, it would be possible
to work out in c njunetion with the U.S. Government
a means of limiting Japan's imports of oil by res-
trictini: Japan's use o: other than Japanese tanker
tonnage.
7.
A detailed menorandun is attached, in order to help in
the examination of Japanese stoclks and consumption, on which the
attention of the U.S.c. is invited to the following particular
points
(1)
Japanese service consumption in the last three years
(2)
Japanese army consunption in the last three years
and the effect of a change oi these estimates upon
(1)
The present Japanese stock position
(2)
The danger of a further accumulation of stocks if
the revised estimate of consunption indicates that
current imports are in excess of consumption.

=== Page 232 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
If an examination of the facts points to the con-
clusion that current Japanese imports are resulting (or
will shortly result) in a further accumulation of stocks,
His Majesty's Government wish to indicate to the U.S. Govern-
ment that in their view the only practical means of dealing
with this very undesirable situation is for the adoption by
both Governments of a joint policy designed to curtail de-
liveries of oil to Japan in non-Japanese tankers or other
vessels.
9.
It is understood that at present no U.S. flag
tanker is engaged in this trade, although oil is carried
as package cargo in U.S. flag freighters. On the other
hand, certain Norwegian and other Allied flag tankers have
been engaged in the Japanese trade. In the event of the
U.S.G. agreeing to a policy of tanker curtailment, H..G.
would refuse licenses to the Japanese for all tankers under
the control of the British or Allied Governments and would
look to the U.S.G. to continue its restrictions upon U.S.
flag tankers and, if possible, to extend similar action
to U.S.-controlled tankers sailing under neutral flags.
10.
The immediate effect of such action would be to
limit Japanese oil imports to the quantities which can be
carried in ordinary freight vessels and in Japanese tankers
and in such free tankers under the Panamanian or perhaps
Swedish flags which might be available. However, H.M.G.
think that it might be possible to arrange long-term
charters for such free tankers to non-Japanese destina-
tions. The detailed effect of such tanker curtailment
upon Japanese imports is cxamined in the attached memo-
randum.
11.

paragraph 9 above would at once be noticed by the
Japanese Government, who might regard it as an
attack upon their vital interests and react for-
cibly, His Majesty's Government would only con-
2
template such action at a stage when they and
the U.3. Governient are agreed as to the point
where accunulation of Japanese stocks of oil

Maritime Comission and the British Ministry of
Shipping would be essential. therwise, a re-
fusal of licenses by the British and Allied
Governments would merely result in an increase
of chartering by the Japanese in the United
States.
(c)
That if restrictions were to be fully effective
they should cover imports into Dairen, Indo-China,
and Thailand, as well as Japan proper. Here
again restrictions may well teke the form of
tanker control through co-operative action with
the U.S.G., and if the U. S. Government are in-
terested in principle His Majesty's Government
will imnediately inform them of the maximum
monthly quantities, if any, for which in their
opinion tankers consigned to these markets
should receive licenses.

To judge from H.M.G.'s estimates of the consumption of oil
by the German Army, the figures in 3.c. for Japanese Army
consumption appear too high. But it should be noted that
the figure for the Japanese Army's consumption in 3.c. 1s
liable to a wide margin of error, since it is a residual
figure, depending for its correctness upon, first the
correctness of the U.S. Navy Department's figure of
service consumption, and second upon the correctness of
the British Service estimates for the consumption by the
Japanese Air Force and Navy in 3.b. above.
It is therefore most important that every endeavour
be made to check the U.S. Navy Department's figure.
If the U.S. Navy Department, after further consideration,
were to lower their estimate of Japan's oil consumption it
follows:
a.
That their figure for present stocks (see 1. above)
is too low.
b.
That existing Japanese contracts will result in a
further accumulation by Japan of oil stocks.
This further accumulation may be prevented by denying to
Japan access to foreign flag tanker tonnage.

425,000 tons.

=== Page 242 === (confidence 0.99)
FOR IMMEDIATE RELEASE
:THE WHITE HOUSE
FOR THE PRESS
SEPTEMBER 26, 1940
CONTROL OF IRON AND STEEL SCRAP EXPORTS
The President has approved the early establishment
of additional controls of the exportation of iron and steel
scrap with a view to conserving the available su rly to m et
the rapidly expanding requirements of the defense program in
this country.
Effective October 15, 1940, all outstanding balances
of licenses which have been granted pursuant to the existing
regulations of July 26, 1940, for the exportation of No. 1
heavy melting steel scrap will be revoked. On October 13,
1940, the exportation of all grades of iron and steel scrap
will be placed under the licensing system.
Under the new regulations which will be made effective
on October 16, 1940, 1icenses will be issued to permit
shipments to the countries of the Western Hemisphere and
Great Britain only.

=== Page 243 === (confidence 0.99)
REGULATIONS GOVERNING THE EXPORTATION OF
ARTICLES AND MATERIALS DESIGNATED IN
THE PRESIDENT'S PROCLAMATION OF
JULY 26, 194O, ISSUED PURSUANT TO THE
PROVISIONS OF SECTION 6 OF THE ACT OF CONGRESS
APPROVED JULY 2, 1940.
Pursuant to the authority vested in me by the
provisions of section 6 of the Act of Congress
approved July 2, 1940, entitled "An Act to expedite
the strengthening of the national defense", I hereby
prescribe the following additional regulations govern-
ing the exportation of:
Iron and Steel Scrap
1. As used in my proclamation of July 26, 1940,
issued pursuant to the provisions of section S of the
Act of Congress approved July 2, 1940, and in the
regulations of July 26, 1940, issued in accordance
with that proclamation, the construction and defini-
tion of the term "iron and steel scrap", is hereby
amended to read:
Iron and Steel Scrap.--All iron and steel
scrap of every kind and description, claesified
or unclassified.
2. Regulations 2 to 12, inclusive, of the regu-
lations iscued on July 2, 1940, pursuant to the ect
of July 2, 1940, are applicable to the exportation of
iron and steel scrap.
3. This regulation shell become effective
October 16, 1940.
FRANKLIN D. ROOSEVELT
THE WHITE HOUSE
September 30, 1940.

=== Page 258 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
November 1, 1940.
EA:
Mr. Trueblood:
The tin and tungsten areas of China
have not been occupted by the Japanese.
The major producing areas for tungsten are
in the province of Kwangtung in South China
and the major producing area for tin 1s in
the province of Yunnan. Except for the
Tungsten which is now moving out over the
Burma Road, Chinese tungsten must make its
way to export markets through the Japanese
lines to Hong Kong and Shanghai, this move-
ment being effected by means of financial
payments to the Japanese military authorities
Although the Japanese do not control the
tungsten producing reas, it is likely that
the Japanese military authorities sre able
to obtain as much of this smuggled tungsten
for their own needs as they wish to take,
except that probably they realize that if
they took a large proportion the flow would
dry up.
Chinese tin has in the past
been exported over the railway through
Indochina but the railway is now severed
and exports are cut off except as they may
make their way over the Burme Road.

offering to put up small sorting plants in

quite see why the Japanese should be so interested
unless it is in getting hold of tungsten. of
course I am still trying to get at the facts
in this matter, and hope to clear up the report
one way or another soon.

much appreciate any information you may be able
to give us regarding the sources from which the
Japanese may now be expected to obtain their
tin and tungsten supplies.
With personal regards, I am,
Cordially yours,

Gentlemen:
We have a contract under which we have
been operating for a period of six or seven years,
with Tatsumi Engineering Company, Ltd., of Tokio,
Japan, which is represented by Mr. Shiro Yamaguchi
who has been in the United States from time to
time in connection with the handling of this work.
This contract contemplates our supplying engineer-
ing design information in connection with petroleum
refining equipment. It is further contemplated
under this contract that the equipment required for
such plants is either to be supplied through pur-
Pol
chase by us in the United States and shipment to
Japan, or fabricated in Japan. As I have told you
in various discussions in the past, since the State
Department's request at the end of 1939 on aviation
C
information and equipment, we have confined ourselves
in our work with Japan solely to simple distillation
units.

-2-
were involved and that the gasoline to be produced by

all gasoline. I said that the probability was that the
distillation units in question would therefore fall within
the terms of the President's proclamation anu it would
seem edvisable for him to apply for an export llcense.

Report 211.
30.10.40.
JAPAN: Recent developments in Petroleum
Situation.
Japan expeots the United States to embargo fuel oils and
lubricating oils within the very near future. Feverish attempts
are therefore being made to inorease the output of these products in
the Japanese refineries. The Tao Nenryo Kogyo K.K.,
Nihon Sekiyu K.K., Nihon Yuka K.K., are all increasing their
output of engine fuels, while the Nihon Sekiyu K.K., Hayayama
Sekiyu K.K., Asahi Sekiyu K.K., Marusen Sekiyu K.K., Mitsubishi
Sekiyu K.K., and the Kogura Sekiyu K.K., are devoting great
attention to an inoreased output of lubrioating oils.
Unfortunately for Japan none of these plants is equipped
to produce muoh of either commodity. And with the stoppage first
of import of machinery and equipment from Europe, and now from the
United States, it is almost impossible to expand the plants to meet
the increased dmand for fuel oils and lubricants.
Since the beginning of 1940 dempite the urgent demand for
all refined petroloum products thare has been a drop in the output
of all Japanese refineries.
Added to refinery troubles Japan has been handicapped in
the import of crude petroleum by a soaroity of tankers. It has
even been mecessary to oharter foreign tankers to keep the supply
of oil flowing from abroad to the Japanese plants. To remedy this
situation the Japanese Govermment is organising a special company
to be known as the MIHON KOKUSAI TANKA K.K. to take complete charge
of the use of tankers by Japanese oil companies.
To compensate for the usual lack of petroleum from the
domestic oil fields, Japen has been pushing forward the construction
of Low Termperature Distillation of coal phants - has increased
subsidies for opening new wells. But there is a lack of steel pipe,
and drilling mmchinery, and domestio mamufacturers of these
mecessities are unahle to cope with the national needs.
/ To

=== Page 302 === (confidence 0.99)
2.
To increase the yield from domestic fields, in July
the Government organized a new sompany, the Teikoku Sekiyu Shigen
Kaihatsu K.X., to carry on prospecting and to develop new oil
fields within Japan, also to take charge of and regulate in its
entirety the operations and outputs of all medium and small sized
oil producing companies without capital to develop their own
petroleum and coal lands.
At the present time there are twelve companies under
the control of the Teikoku Nenryo Kogyo K.K., all engaged in
undertaking the low temperature distillation of coal. of these
seven companies are now getting down to business of operation,
four of them are actively producing coal - and it is planned that
they will all be operating at capacity by the end of 1942- 1943.
of these seven operating companies the most interesting
at the present moment is the NIHON YUKA K.Kc (Oil Conversion Co.Ltd)
It is a Mitsui subsidiary, and uses the Fischer-Tropach process
for whioh Mitsui & Co., own the Japanese and Manchurian rights;
it also uses the I.G. process.
This company has installed the Knowles Coal Furnaces,
and is obtaining coke and tar from coal; but this has been a
fairly wasteful procedure, so that the company is now re-distilling
the produot and getting Benzol and Naptha, as well as Napthalene and
Carbolic Acid (Phenol)., and Crosol. Production is amall.
Actual
figures are not to be obtained, but as the daily output of the coal
furnaces is not over 50 tons, the output of end products cannot be
very great.
Furthermore the deficit in coal output does not permit
of much low temperature distillation of coal at this time. These
plants are more or less 'stand-by' plants; and probably never will
be of great importance in the Japanese petroleum and chemical
industries even under 'emergenoy' conditions.

November 15, 1940.
MEMORANDUM
Confidential
The following information regarding the
production of zinc in Colombia appeared in a letter
written in Japanese from I. Okura, Bogota, to Mitsui
894.24/11
Bussan Kaisha, Ltd., Tokyo:
There is a zinc mine of superior
quality in the vicinity of Bogota City.
Dr. Shimizu, who is now in Bolivia, cal-
led here last year and guaranteed its
superiority of quality, the ore containing
from 55 to 64% metal.
6
Owing to the low market price of
zinc and the inconvenience of transporta-
tion, exportation from this mine was inac-
tive. The Nippon Soda of Japan bought 150
tons from here in 1928 but, due to the high
price, has not bought any since.
There are about 1,Oo0 tons stored
at present and r. rnesto Median, the owner
of the mine, is quite willing to sell zinc
ore to Japan proviaed a reasonable price is
offered. I have sent you two bags of sample
zinc ore. Please examine them carefully and
look for prospective buyers.
Here is an assay given by Mr. Median

OMMUNICATIONS
AND RECORDS
MR. BERLE
Bubject:
Cargoes loaded by Japanese vessels at the port of New
Orleans, Louisiana.
Summary of Information:
Japanese vessels loading at the port of New Orleans are taking
considerable cargoes of gasoline, crude oil and lubricating oil in
metal drums. Reports indicate that formerly most of these cargoes
were probably taken in bulk, whereas at the present time they are
all taken in metal drums, most of which are new.
Information indicates that the purchase price includes both
the drum and its contents in each case. The drums apparently become
the property of the purchaser, indicating the possibility that they
are not to be returned to this country.
The following is a summary of the cargoes taken by Japanese ves-
sels departing New Orleans, Louisiana, since 1 July, 1940:

=== Page 380 === (confidence 0.97)
TREASURY DEPARTMENT
UNITED STATES COAST GUARD
WASHINGTON 16 Docember, 1940.
Subject: Gorman SS PAULINE FRIEDERICH.
Summary of Information:
It is reported that the John G. Hall Company, Steamship Agents,
Boston, Massachusetts, have been approached with a view to having
that concern offer subject vessel for sale. The attorney for the
owmers in these negotiations is reported to be Mr. Arthur P. Teele,
45 Milk Street, Boston, Massachusetts, who does not ordinarily do
business for the German Consul. The reported reason for this
attempted sale is that German and Italian authorities fear seizure
of their vessels in American ports.
It is understood that the price asked for the vessel is $300,000.
As far as is known no offers to purchase the vessel have been made.
DECLASSIFIED

Washington, D. C.
Dear Senator Bankhead:
We approve our Government's increased financial aid to
China and ask that effective embargoes be placed on ALL
war supplies to Japan at once. We urge especially that
no American company, whether in the United States or the
Dutch East Indies, be allowed to furnish any grade of oil
or gasoline to Japan.
We resent the fact that a few dealers in war materials
have been allowed to make this country a partner in cruelty
that fills us with horror, in a policy that will close our
mission work and shut American business out of the Orient,
and in a war that seeks to overthrow democracy.
We make this plea in the interest of humanity, of our na-
tional honor, and of our personal safety.
Respectfully yours,
(Signed)
Miss Gladys Thomas
56 Main Blvd.,
Craft View Camp,
Pricnard, Alabama.
and signed by other members of Prichard, Alahama.

Ba el Mumicipio de Junin, vereda de Playas, Departemeste
de Oandinsmeres, se conoce desde hace algunos anos un yeelmiem
te de blanda de sino. EIn la region en donde aflora la vete sele
e emeuentran rocss sedimentarias de eded cretecee entre las eseles
desime el esquisto arailloso negro y gris osoure, la calise y al
games bancos de arenisce pertenecientes al piso de villets. la ve-
ta se halle asociade con siderita, calcopirita, pirita y arsemepi-
rite y solo se puede observar un afloramiento "in situ", donde el
espesor de la propia veta de blenda aloansa acerce de un metre.
Alli puede observarse que la veta se halla asociada con une falle.
In otro sitio, se encontraron abundantes riegoe de bloques
grandes de blenda y en otro una veta de siderita con calcopirita,
pirita, arsenopirita y galena, como minerales accesorios. Como
los tres lugares se hallan sensiblemente sobre una linea recta.
cuyo rumbo coincide con el de la veta en su afloramiento, e he
creido conveniente indicar la verificacion de algunos trabajos
subterraneos para constatar si todas las muestras recogidas en
esos lugares provienen de une sola veta, que no aflore mas que
en un punto se la superficie. En caso de que esto llegare a de
mostrarse, tal veta tendria una extension superficial de ceroa
de 3 kilometros y una cuelga suficiente para justificar su ex-
plotacion comercial.

La region se helle inteneamente percelada y cultivace y

veja, la arracacha, la yuca y la cana de azucar.
El sitio llamado "El Rincon", dnde aflora la veta de blenda,
se halla unido con Gacheta, Gama y Junin por buenos caminoe de
herradura. Por lo demas, toda la region se hallu atravesade por
numerosos caminos y senderos que presentan buenas facilidades
para el trafico de bestias. La poblacion de Gacheta se halla uni-
da a Bogota por una carretera de 90 kilometros de longitud. Del
centro de la concesion a Gacheta podria, sin mayores dificultades,

ESTRATIGRAFIA.
Como ya se dijo, la totalidad de la region estudiada se ha-
lle cubierta por rocas sedimentarias. Estratigraficamente, todos
loe terreno  tienen la misme constitucion geologica; solamemte a

dos pisos de Villete y de Guadalupe, en los estudios geologioos
oficiales llevados a cabo hesta la fecha.
Pioo do villeta.
El pise de Villeta forma la mayor parte de los terrenos a
barcados por la concesion, como puede verse en el mapa de la plan-
che No. 2. Eeenctalmente se hallu compuesto de bancos dominantes
de esquistos arcillosos negros y grises con alternacion de ban-
cos de calisa fosilifera y bancos secundarios de arenisca cuar-
citica blanca o rojisa. Los esquistos arcillosos alternan con
lechos delgados de arenisca y en general se descomponen facil-

Depositos Aluviales recientes.

acarreo cuaternario de la boca de la quebrada Negra, por tener
importancia economica toda vez que la mayor parte de sus bloques
son de blenda de sinc.

ESTRUCTURA
La estructura de la region es bastante compleja y su des-
cifraccion se dificulto bastante, tanto por la ausencia de bue-
nos horizontes guias y de buenos afloramientos, como por el he-
cho de que los sedimentos del piso de Villeta son tan homogeneos
que en la mayor parte de los casos donde se observaron fallas,
resulto dificil determinar el movimiento relativo de los bloquee,
que despues de haber sido afectadoe por la erosion presentan 
racteres litologicos muy semejantes.
Al este del rio Sucio, los estratos tienen un rumbo general
noreste y se inclinan hecia el oriente con angulos variables en-
tre 20 y 60 grados. Al occidente de dicho rio las inclinaciones
varian mucho, pero prevalece un buzamiento, hacia el occidente,
lo que demuestra la presencia de un gran anticlinal que sigue
en terminos generales al lado oriental del curso de dicho rio,
y que por eso lo llemaremos del rio Sucio. En el flanco occidon-
tal de este anticlinal se encontraron las muestras de blenda y
por ese motivo se estudio mas on detalle. Alli se pueden obser-
var varias fallas entre lae cuales se destaca la de "Em Rincon",
cuya importancia economica es sobresaliente puesto que con ella
se halla asociado el unico afloramiento de blend que se pudo
observar. Unès 500 metros al Suroeste de la casa de Benigno
Léon, en "El Rincon", se obeervo cleramente la exposicion de
la falla que se ilustra en la figura No. 2, y en las fotogra-
fias Nos. 7, 8 y 9.
Igualmente la presencia de una brecha de friccion, asi
como de condiciones estructurales retorcidas y superficies de
deslizamiento (elikensides) en el afloremiento de la veta de
zinc de "El Rincon", demuestran la presencia de una falla.
Aunque no fue posible determinar exactemente su rumbo sobre el
terreno, se ha considerado que se trate de la misma falla ob-
servada al suroeste de "El Rincon" y ue ella se prolongue he-
cia el norte y sur y sea la que sirve de control estructural
a la distribucion de la mineralizacion que se estudio. La pre
sencia de esta falla se evidencia, ademas, por la falta de se-
dimentos del Barremiense, que se constato mediante el estudio
paleontologico de que se hablo atras. Parece que se trata de una
falla de angulo al to, que probablemente tiene el caracter de
sobreescurrimiento. Sin embargo esta caracteristica igualmente
debe consideraree como provisional, puesto que el plano propiemente
dicho no se observo y esto solo se dijo de la posicion de las ro-
oas en el afloramiento de "El Rincon". Dicha falla se halla aso-
ciada con otras dislocaciones mas pequenas que se observaron a
ambos lados de la linea que ella sigue.
Hacia el occidente de esta falla, en el lecho del rio Sucio,
se observo el afloramiento de la figura No. 3, que demuestra la
prosencia de otra falla cuyas caracteristicas no pudieron deter-
minarse exactamente, debido a que eolo se obeervo en el sitio in.
dioado.
Al occidente de la falla de "El Rincon" igualmente se ob
servaron condiciones estructurales muy retorcidas y la presensia
de dislocaciones menores, que no fueron estudiadas en detalle

de zinc de "E? Rincon". Estos tres afloramientos se hallan lo-
calizados en la plancha No. 2.

La zona No. 1 no se halla bien delimitada y se puede observar

sociadas con la blenda y la siderita, mientras que la pirita se
halla asociada tanto con los minerales como con las vetillas de
cuarso, lo que hace pensar que existan piritas de dos genera
ciones.
El espesor de la veta de zinc propiamente dicha varía de

Corte superior.
En el corte superior del afloramiento de "El Rincon" se

arcillosa en parte mineralizada por blenda calcopirita y pirita.
 desarrollo vertical de la veta es bastante variable; en
la distancia de unos dos metros que se destaparon, la banda cen-
tral se extingue hacia arriba, la del lado occidental se adel-
gaza considerablemente, mientras que la oriental conserva mas o
menos su mismo espesor. Esto viene a poner de presente el carac-
ter lenticular de la veta y la irregularidad de la distribucion
de la blenda dentro de ella, no solo horizontal sino vertical~
mente. Igualmente, esta obeervacion viene a demostrar la nece-
sidad de estudiar sus caracteristicas en diferentes lugares an-
tes de hacer una prospeccion definitiva.

En una sona de unos 200 metros comprendida entre el rio
Sacio y la quebrada Negra, de la boca de esta bacia arribe, se
encuentra en el acarreo de la ladera una sorprendente concen-
tracion de bloques grandes redondeados de blenda de sine, fun-
to con siderita y los mismos sulfuros que se describieron en
el afloramiento de "El Rincon", situado topografioamente 350
metros mas al to y a una distancia de 1, 750 metros con rumbo
8 38 W. Mineralogicamente, los bloques tienen la misma compo-
sicion de la veta observada en el "Rincon". l caracter de la
blenda es identico y la asociacion de minerales la miama; es
decir, ganga de siderita y acompanamiento secundario de piri-
ta, calcopirita y vetillas de cuarzo. La unica partáoularidad
observada es la presencia de cristales de blenda roja crista-
lizada en la superficie de algunos bloques y el caracter alta-
mente hematitico de la siderita. Puede afirmarse sin ricsgo de
quivocacion que e1 95% de los bloques del acarreo de esa zona
estan formados por blenda con la asociacion de que se acaba de
habler. Semejante concentracion tan abundante de bloquee, aun-
que son redondeadoe, no puede menos de indicar que provienen
de un sitio cercano, y puesto que mineralogioamente tienen i-
gual composicion y asociasion al material de la veta de "El
Rimcon", logioamente puede suponerse que provengan de la mi
ma veta, o por lo menos de otra veta que tenga el mismo ori-
gen y sea contemporanea con la dd "El Rincon".
Los blocues varian en tamano de 30 centimetros a 1 metro
de diametro y la hematizacion de la siderita no es tan consi-
derable para suponer que hayan sufrido un transporte largo.
El unico hecho que puede ser desfavorable para esta teoria es
el redondeamiento de todos los bloques. Sin embargo, esto pue-
de explicarse por la fragilidad de la blenda que aun con poco
transporte puede redondearse. Sin embargo, a pesar de las ex-
cavaciones hechas tanto por el concesionario como por la Co-
mision, no se pudo descubrir la veta in-situ.
Afloramiento de Llano Grande.
En el sitio de "Llano Grande", situado a unos 1,400 metros
al suroeste de "El Rincon" sobre el camino que va a la vereda
de Arrayanes, se observaron varias monas de concentracion de
material altamente limotizado, que se creyo proveniente de la

sitios se hicieron pequenas exoavaciones para comprobar esta
idea, obteniendo resultadoe negativos, pues pronto se llegaba
a los esquistos frescos o a los bancos de calisa que solo su-
perficialmente se hallaban limonitizados, seguramente por so-
luciones ferrigimosas acarreadas por aguas superficiales. Solo
en un corte profundo del camino, situado en el punto que se in-
dioa en la plancha No. 2, se obruvieron resultados satisfactorios.
Alli se hizo una excavacion de unos 2.50 metros de profundidad
y en ella se observo que la costra de limonita pronto se conver-
tia en dderita en forma de veta, como puede observarse en la fi-
gura No. 8.
La veta tiene un rumbo de N 10 E y se inclina con 40 a 50
grados al occidente. El espesor observado fue aproximadamente
0.80 metros y se enouentra emplasada sensiblemente entre los
planos de estretificacion de los esquistos arcillosos negros y
grises y las arenisoas algo cuarciticas de la parte media del

esta cempuesta de siderita pure, algo hematísada, pero que con-
