Origen de la Mineralizacion.
Los depositos comerciales de blenda de sinc del mundo, se
encuentran en una variedad de tipos de yacimientos, entre los
cuales merecen mencionarse los siguientes: 1) - En verdaderas
vetas metaliferas. 2) - En masas irregulares en rocas metamor-
ficas. 3) - In masss irregulares o teninaciones formadas por
substitucion o impregnadon en calizas o cuarcitas. 4) - m yaci-
mientos de metemorfiemo de contacto. 5) - mn cantidades que no
corresponden altipo de vetas de relleno, y 6)-  arcillas resk
duales.
EL YAOIMIENTO

=== Page 422 === (confidence 0.97)
I yacimiento que estudiamos, no tiene definitivamente les
carecteristicas de ninguno de los tipos. se aproxime ms a de
"verdadera veta matalifera", aunque su forma no es exmctamente
la de una veta, o cuerpo tabular de forma sensiblemente geome.
trice que dgue una fracture rellenada por material mas o me
nos uniforme, sino que se trata de una verdadera sona de mine-
ralizacion dentro de la cual, la ganga, los minerales y el me-
terial de los respaldos se hallan distribuidos irregularmente
en forma de masas y lentes.
La descifracion del verdadero origen de un yacimiento de
esta clase, tiene una importancia economica inmediata, pues so-
lo a base de su estudio puede predecirse con algun fundamento,
las condiciones de mineralisacion que pueden esperarse a lo
largo y a la profundidad de la veta en cuestion. Por este motivo
nos extenderemos algo en la discusion de este punto.
Dos teorias se han sugerido para explicar yacimientos de
zinc semejantes al presente, y ambas han sido ampliemente dis
cutidas por eminentes autoridades geologicas, especialmente en
relacion con los yacimientos de plomo y zine de Joplin, Mismouri,
Estados Unidos, que, como se sabe, son los mas gran des del mun-
do. La primera de estas teorias atribuye la concentracion de es-
tos sulfuros aa accion de agua subterranea meteorica descenden-
te. La segundabostula que fueron transportados y depositados por
medio de agua magmatice, caliente, cargada de emamaciones igneas.
En el primer caso la mineralizacion solo puede esperarse que se
extienda hasta la profundidad normalmente aloanzada por las a
guas meteoricas descendentes; en el segundo hasta el cuerpo mie-
mo de la roca ignea que la origina. De aqui la importancia de
solucionar el problema del origon.
La teoria de deposicion por egua meteorica tropiesa con al-
gunas dificultades pues el papel desempenado por la circulacion
profunda de las aguas subterraneas en la ancentracion de yaci-
mientos minerales esta menos claramente demostrada que la mayor
parte de los otroe procesos de deposicion mineral. Hace algunos
anos, cuando la teoria de la segregacion lateral se tenia por s
gura, la importancia de la circulacion profunda de agua subterra-
nea se tenia casi fuera de discusion, pero ahora, que la mayoria
de los yacimientos metaliferos originados debajo de la capa de
meteorizacion han sido explicados convincentemente como origina-
dos por la accion de aguas magmaticas, la accion de las aguas me-
teoricas ha quedado cada vez mas descartada. Ademas, ha sido com-
probado que es mucho menor de lo que se creie en un principio y,
en todo caso menor de la me esoapaz de llevar en solucion una
agua magmatica cargada de mineralizadores de origen igneo.
Al estudiar detenidamente las caracteristicas mineralogi-
cas y estructurales del yacimiento de Playas, se tiene que lle
gar a la conclusion que el proceso mineralisador que er efectuo
no se llevo a cabo por medio del proceso de deposicion por agua
meteorica, sino por agua magmatioa caliente, a pesar de la for-
ma mas o menos irregular de la veta y la ausencia de rocas igneas
o diques pegmatitioos en la vecindad y la falta de propilitiza-
cion de las rocas de los respaldos.
a favor de la deposicion por agua magmatica pueden citarse
los sguientes rasgos del yacimiento:
a) - La

d) - Sequito de vetas de cuarzo con sulfuros.
La presencia de arsenopirita, asi como la falta de paredes
bien definidas y de crustificacion en la veta, indican qie pro-
bablemente se trata de un yacimiento formado en la zona llamada
por Lddgrem mesotermal, a una profundidad intermedia y a tempe-
ratura moderada; es decir algo asi como de 200 a 300 grados 0.
Lo que oscurece un poco las verdaderas caracteristicas, fuera
de la meteorizacion de loe afloremicntos actuales, es el reem-
plazo metasomatico que se efectuo dentro de la veta propiamente
dicha y en las paredes de ella por accion de las emanaciones de
la solucion hidrotermal. La paragenesis de los minerales resul
ta un poco incierta, y su descifracion requeria el estudio mi-
croscopico de algunas secciones, los cual se considera inutil
para los fines de este estudio. Parece que la blenda es poste-
rior a la siderita y los otros sulfuros posteriores a la blen-
da; y por fin el quarzo y la pirita que lo acompane posteriores
a esta mineralizacion.
En todo caso, quedando comprobado el origen magmatico de la
mineralizacion, existe base suficiente para juzgar que esta se
extienda hacia abajo hesta la masa ignea o sus pegmatitas, de
la cual la veta que se observo debe ser un apofisis. Lo que pue-
de suceder es que el batolito igneo aunque no eflora en ningu-
na parte se halla situado a una profundidad relativamente baja
de las roces que forman el subsuelo que hoy se observa.
A este respecto creemos oportuno poner de presente que 0.
Stutmer (Bibl. 2, pag. 154) dice que en los rios que descien-
den hacia Medina y Villavicencio se encuentran rodados de gre-
nito y que en los Farrallones existe un porfiro sienitico, que
nosotros consideramos que pueden venir del batolito con el cual
se relaciona la mineralizacion que se estudia.
Control estructural.

a raiz de loe movimientos diastroficos que la originarom. La fa

su definicion en el terreno resulta bastante dificil por la fal

litologicos de los sedimentos en ella envueltos. Asi pues, la
linea de la falla trasada en el plano, no puede considerarse
absolutemente definitiva y requiere definirse mejor, a base de
estudios estratigraficos detallados o, indirectamente a base de
la localizacion de la veta que debe rellener le fisura de le fe-
11a.

FACILIDADES PARA UNA FUTURA EXPLOTACION.
A) - Vias de comunicacion.
Del sitio de "El Rincon" al puente de Gacheta o a un pun-
to apropiado sobre el rio Cacheta, caso de instalar una planta
de beneficio en ese sitio, podria construirse sin mayor costo
un cable aereo que tendria una longitud maxima de 8 y 5 kilo-
metros respectivemente, si en el segundo caso se escogiera la
boom del rio Sacio.
Como ya se dijo del Puente de Gacheta a Bogota hay una dis-
TANCIA

=== Page 428 === (confidence 0.97)
tansie de s9 kilämetros por ums buena carretera.
Case de que se pensare exportar el mineral, de Bogote pe-
dria transportarse dreetemente a Buenaventure por ferroearril,
con un trayecto de unos 90 kilometros de carretera entre Ibegue
y Armenia, o bien de Bogota a Puerto Salgar en el ferrocarril
de Cundinamaroa y de alli a Barranquilla por el rio Magdalens.
B) - mersia.
A juzgar por la cantidad de agua llevada por el rio Sucio
durante la epoca de nuestra visita, que correspondio a una 
quia, puede suponer que el rio tiene un gasto de 400 litros por
segundo, quo aprovechando una caida de 300 metros producirian
1,200 caballos de fuerza.
Igualmente existe la posibilidad de aprovechar el caudal
del rio Gacheta, que con un gasto de 2,0oo litros por segundo
y con una caida de 100 metros suministraria 2,000 caballos de
fuerza.
g) - Combuatible.
Carbon mineral del tipo de hulla, se dice que existe en
las cercanias de la salina de dacheta, distante unos 15 kilo-
metros del sitio de "El Rincon". Caso de que este yacimiento
no pudiera suministrar el carbon necesario, podria penearse en
traerlo de la region de Guasca-Guatavita, que tiene abundantes
reservas y que se halla unida a Gacheta por un tramo de carre
tera no mayor de 60 kilometros.
D) - Obreros y abustecimiento.
Como ya se dijo, la region se halla intensamente poblada
y cultivada. Alli miamo podrian conseguirse tanto los obreros
como los viverem indispensables.
CONCLUSIONES.
En vi sta de todes lae consideraciones hechas en el curso
de este informe, opinamos que aunque el yacimiento de zinc de
Playas no permite ser apreciado en su verdadero valor con los
afloramientos actuales, si justifica una inversion para com-
prender la exploracion tecnica.
Bogota, junio de 1940.
Benjamin Alvarado.
Jefe de la Comision de Estudios Mineros.
Climaco Palau,
Ingeniero Ayudante.

Sir:
Reference is made to your telephone call of Decem-
ber 2lst to the Supervising Customs Agent in connection
with an investigation to obtain further data as to cer-
tain shipments of cobalt ore concentrates from Canada
to the Metal & Ore Corporation of this city, which were
subsequently sold to Mitsui & Company for shipment to
Japan.
Under date of November 29, 1940 our report dealt
with the ore covered by:
Newark Entry No. 064 dated 10/8/40 -
118 bags
Newark Entry No. 065 dated 10/8/40 -
135 bags
125
total we1ght - 215,790 pounds.
In the above-mentioned report the disposition of the
cobalt concentrates covered by Newark Entry No. O92
dated November 7, 1940, consisting of one lot - 127 bags,
and 100 bage, total weight 119,996 pounds, was not 1nves-
tigated.
You are advised that the collector's records show
that 227 bags arsenical cobalt ore, weighing 124,030
pounds, covered by Export Declaration No. 537047, was
oleared from New York November 16, 1940, addressed to:
Mitsu1 Buesan Kaisha Ltd.,
Tokyo, Japan,
and

=== Page 438 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
and was shipped on the "Atutasan Maru", the mark being
有
K 15
12
Mitsui & Co.
1/227
Osaka
Under even date the undersigned agents visited the
office of the Metal & Ore Corporation at 233 Broadway,
New York City, and interviewed Mr. Joseph J. Haesler,
vioe-president. When questioned as to the above-mentioned
shipments, Mr. Haesler readily admitted that they had been
sold to Mitsui & Company and had been shipped to Japan.
In asking him for details regarding the contract for the
sale to Mitsui as to the original order covering the cobalt
ore concentrates, he produced the correspondence relative
to this transaction.
According to this correspondence with fifteen concerns,
including the General Eleotric Company, E. I. DuPont
de Nemours Company, Calco Company, the Metal & Ore Corpora-
tion endeavored to have the ore from Canada refined in this
country. From the replies received it is evident that the
domestic concerns were either not equipped to do the re-
fining or else were engaged in other operations, which pre-
cluded them from handling the cobalt ores.
As a last resort they contacted Mr. John Sullivan of
the Sulmet Alloys Company, Inc., of Clifton, N. J., who
had recently enlarged their plant. In fact, a 200 pounds
sample order was furnished the latter named company with
the thought in mind as to their refining the ore.
Mr. Sullivan stated that it would be physically 1mpos-
sible for him to handle it as his plant was not suitable
either in equipment or location; that the free arsenic
would go off as smoke and would settle about the surround-
ingcommunity and on that account it was prohibited to
handle the refining.
At this point Mr. Haesler was asked if any actual
orders had been given for the ore in question. He stated
that no aciual orders had been made but there was a
gentlemen's agreement between his firm and the C. Sterling
Smith Company of Detroit, Mich., and Windsor, Ontario, for
the ore under discussion, and also an additional 150 tons,
which the last named company was holding for the Metal &
Ore Corporation's order. At the time it was found that
the Sulmet Alloys, Inc. of Clifton, N. J., positively re-
fused to refine any order and as he wiehed to continue
his negotiations with C. Sterling Smith of Detroit to
purohase the ore in question, he informed Mr. C. Sterling
Smith as to the faots and that the only way for him to die-
pose of the ore would be to sell it for export to Japan or,
as he stated, to the same source as "your shipment about
a month ago"; that there was no question but that C. Sterling
Smith knew of the disposition of the ore because there had
been

=== Page 439 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
been several telephone calls relative to the shipment of
this ore to Japan and further that he considered that he,
Haesler, deemed it in no way irregular because of the
fact that the C. Sterling Smith Company, who handled nearly
the entire product of the ore from Cobalt, Ontario, had
shipped hundreds of tons of this same ore directly to
Japan and that a shipment as above mentioned had gone for-
ward about a month previous to the shipment of the ores
imported by the Metal & Ore Corporation.
It was also established that the contract was made
with Mitsui & Company on the basis of "ex warehouse
Jersey City" on September 1l, 1940.
On September 13, 1940 an irrevocable letter of credit
was opened by Metal & Ore Corporation through the Chase
National Bank of New York in favor of the Smith Brokerage
Company of Detro1t, Mich., for $23,000.
Mr. Haesler admitted that the sale to Mitsui and
Company had been coneummated prior to the date that the
goods were ordered shipped from Canada and that Mr. C.
Sterling Smith was aware of the ultimate destination of
the ore.
Mr. Haesler further informed the undersigned agents
that he had been placed on the "black liet" by Canada
and as he stated, it was never his intention to do other
wise than have the cobalt concentrates refined in this
country and the only reason for his selling the two ship-
ments under discussion to Mitsui was that he wished to
protect his arrangement with C. Sterling Smith as he felt
sure that he could arrange to have the ore refined in this
country and sell it domestically.
Mr. Haesler showed the undersigned agents a copy of
a contract with the Navy Department for tungsten ore for
an estimated price of $438,000 and a copy of a letter from
the Naval Purchasing Office to a member of the Naval Com-
mittee in Congress, stating that the contract had been
fulfilled within the specified time and in a satisfactory
manner.

COBALT SHIPMENTS TO JAPAN
The following matter was brought to my attention
first by the Defense Commission and was subsequently
894.24/1200
confirmed in detail by Mr. Marris of the British Embassy:
The Barber Line ship Westhumaw (United States flag)
1s expected to arrive in Boston on November 5 and will
proceed to New York, arriving there on about November 9,
1940. This ship has a cargo of 426 metric tons of
orude cobalt consigned to Mitsui and lo5 tons of the
51
same material consigned to Mitsubishi, both of which
Ps/
shipments presumably will be forwarded to Japan as
soon as possible. This cargo 1s from the Belgian Congo.
FF
894.2
The cobalt included in this one shipment is equiva-
lent to approximately a year's sunply for Japan. Japa-
nese stocks, however, are understood to be relatively
high and hence the suspicion arises that this cobalt is
GRADY
or State
actually destined for Germany. In any event it is an
NON
exceptionally large quantity of a highly strategio
material (eesential in the manufacture of machine tools).
United States supplies of cobalt are fairly large
and there evidently is no immediate strategic reason
for aoguiring the shipment 1n question. Nevertheless
1t
1200

=== Page 459 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
it might be obeerved that the United States draws
heavily upon the Belgian Congo for its supplies and the
United Kingdom, according to Mr. Marris, does not wish
to interrupt this trade. Should tnis shipment to Japan
go through, however, it is very probable that the
British Embassy here would be inclined to recommend that
Belgian Congo trade with the United States be stopped.
In view of this factor, if for no otner reason, considera-
tion mignt be given to embargoing this particular cargo.

Exports of gasoline to Japan increased very haterially
durint tre l st four months of l940. Durthmfsthe years

barrels of gsoline were exported to Japan each year. In
894.24/
1940 nearly 3, 500,000 barrels were ex orted, of which
nearly 3,000,000 were exported in the last four months of
the year and 1, C C, oc in the nonth of December alone.
Approximately 7, 000,C00 barrels of soline have been
1200
licensed for ex ort to Japan betveen J 1y 2c, 1940, when
the licensing system was applied to gasoline, and Janu-
4/5
ary 15, 1941. 0f this 7,000,000 barrels 11censed, approx-

high-quality evit'n g eoline havine an octane number

above e7. ost of she  soline exported n ilcensed for.
S
/MFM
export to Japan in the l st six monthe hes been of a medium
avietion quality havine n ettne nuraber betreen se and s7.
This

=== Page 472 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
This gasoline can be used with satisfactory results in
most Japanese military planes and, moreover, can by a very
simple process be converted into high-quality aviation
gasoline. It should be noted that many of the applica-
tions presented for license to export gasoline to Japan
state that the gasoline is for aviation purposes and that
it 1s for delivery to the Imperial Japanese Navy or Army.
The exportation of crude oil to Japan was less in
1940 than in recent previous years, having amounted to
about 14,000,000 barrels 1n 1940, 18,000,000 1n 1939, and
21,000,000 1n 1938. However, about 20,000,000 barrels
have been licensed for export and of this amount about
15,000,000 remain to be shipped.
Exports of the types of g&soline and crude oil sub-
ject to license (about 9c of al1 gasolines and 50% of all
crude oils) could be prohibited or restricted at any time
by a simple directive from the Administrator of Export
Control. Such a directive could either, as the Adminis-
trator has on several occasions recommended, lower to
approximately 80 the octane number of the gasoline which
can not be exported to Japan or restrict exports on both
gasoline and crude oil to Japan to usual pre-war amounts.
The latter would necessarily and the former presumably
involve the revocation of a large proportion of the out-
standing licenses.
In

=== Page 473 === (confidence 0.98)
3
In this connection it should be pointed out that
the Administrator in a general revision of all the export
regulations which he is now preparing proposes to subject
to the licensing requirement all or most of the petroleum
products which are not at present subject to that require-
ment. He would appreciate having the Department's reaction
to this proposed step.
II
A subsidiary question to be considered in connection
with the export of petroleum products to Japan is that of
the export of metal drums, storage tanks, and oil well
machinery and drilling equipment.
A very large proportion of the gasoline going forward
to Japan ie shipped in metal drums. The possession of these
drums affords the Japanese a very convenient means of stor-
ing gasoline and transporting it from place to place. The
quantity of iron and steel being drained out of the country
in the form of these drums is also of some significance.
The Japanese purchases of steel storage tanks in this coun-
try have increased greatly in recent weeks and have reached
large proportions. The same would appear to be true of oil
well drilling equipment. The British have informed us on
several occasions that all three of these commodities are
reaching Germany by way of Japan and have urged that we
subject them to the licensing requirement.
The

=== Page 474 === (confidence 0.97)
-4
The Administrator of Export Control has made a care-
ful study of the matter and has informed us that these
three commodities cannot be subjected to the licensing
requirement without a further executive order by the
President. This could be done either by the issuance of
a separate executive order naming these three commodities
alone, which could be issued in a few days, or by their
inclusion in the revision of the general regulations
referred to above, which it is believed will be presented
to the President in about ten days and will become effec-
tive about three weeks thereafter.
Charles Wyost
Co:CWY:IRV

Co-m.

BY TIPTON. M. D.
E. TEBROCK. M. D.
Mr Henry L. Stimson,
1
Secretary of War,
Washington, D. C.
My dear Mr. Stimson:-
I am greatly concerned over the reports from our
Departments of Commerce that Japan 1s buying heavily of
gasoline supplies in the United States and while this is
not aviation type gasoline, the Japanese have plants of
distilleries in Japan in which they can readily convert
commercial gasoline into aviation gasoline. Why are we
allowing this condition to exist, when the gasoline we
are shipping to Japan will soon be used against us in
their planes.
I am also in receipt of information that while Japan
is not able to buy scrap iron in this country, she can buy
finished armor plate and has placed orders for considerable
amount of this plate which we ourselves need very badly, and
even if we did not need it, what manner of common sense is
there that dictates this Government to allow Japan to pur-
chase armor plate from this country to protect their ships
against future shells which we will be forced to fire against
her. If there is anything that could be sillier than our
exporting gasoline and armor plate to Japan, I would like to
know what it is.
Also, according to commercial reports, Mexico is now
receiving from the United States, scrap iron and oil which
everyone knows Mexico is not going to use, but simply trans-
shipping it to Japan. If that isn't a siily method of beating
the devil around the bush what is it. It is high time that
the Government at Washington used ordinary common sense in
these matters, and I am writing to you as a man who is pre-
dominant in this country as a man with unusual common sense ,
to please answer these questions for me; they seem so elementary
that even a grammar school child would have an answer.
Appreciating an explanation from your department on
these vital questions, I am,
Very truly yours,
Eongeting
George SKing, M. D

December 6, 1940.
Mr. Homnbeck:
U--Mr. Welles:
M Sooretary:
There is attached a letter dated
November 30, 1940, from Mr. Roger S. Greene
of the Committee to Defend America by Aiding
the Allies, enclosing a copy of a circular
to the Board of Trustees of Yenching
University, Peiping, China, from Dr. J.Leighton
Stuart, president of that institution.
Dr. Stuart reports the heartiest
endorsement of Americans in north China of
the American action in reetricting scrap
iron exports to Japan. He states: "We hope
that it will be extended to other war
materials, or even to complete stoppage of
all imports from and exports to Japan until
there is a fundamentel change of policy in
that country. This would mean concretely
the withdrawal of all their armed forces
from Chine and an abandonment of the southward
or oceanic expansion. Should this involve us
1n war with Japan--as I still think 1s un-
l1kely--it would be more quickly end easily
over with than if allowed to drift until the
inevitable and far more disastrous conflict
of the future, when the moral aspects of our
action would also be less easy to defend."
Three

=== Page 480 === (confidence 0.98)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-2-
Three possible Japanese courses are
foreseen by Dr. Stuart: (1) "A reckless
continuance of the imperialistio aggression
the extent of which is now being unmistakably
disclosed";(2) retrenchment and ultimate
abandonment of Japanese expansion;(3) "com-
pliance with Amerioan demands, with fair
promises and some minor concessions, thus
attempting to lull our people into a com-
fortable belief that the crisis is over.
. . . As one who hates war as ardently as
can any pacifist, whose life also and almost
all that makes life desirable would be in
immediate danger if either of the other
courses were adopted by Japan, I fear this
dilatory opportuniam or illusory quieting
down of American sentiment more than anything
else that could happen. For it can only
lead to vastly greater wretchedness for
China, far heavier cost to ourselves later,
and tragedy for Japen."
Finally, Dr. Stuart states: "The chief
purpose in my writing again 1s to plead as
passionately as possible that our Government
and people see this thing through at any
cost, now that they have taken a definite
stand, regardless of plausible Japanese
publicity or meritricious proposals of
compromise. For these will not be and indeed
cannot be genuine. Their stakes in this
adventure are too heavy."

To the Board of Trustees:
There is the heartiest endorsement here among Americans gen-
erally over the action of our Government in prohititing the sale of
steel and scrap-iron to Japan. We are also encouraged by what seems to
be widespread editorial and popular approval of this decision. We hope.
that it will be extended to other war materials, or even to complete
stoppage of all imports from and exports to Japan until there is a fun-
damental change of policy in that country. This wculd mean concretely
the withdrawal of all their armed forces from China and an abandonment
of the southward or oceanic expansion. Should this involve us in war
with Japan - as I still think is unlikely - it would be more quickly and
easily over with than if allowed to drift until the inevitable and far
more disastrous conflict of the future, when the moral aspects of our ac-
tion would aiso be less easy to defend.
Japan has now three pcssible courses in response to the Amer-
ican attitude:
(1) A reckless continuance of the imperialistic arrression
the extent of which is now being unmistakably disolosed. This would be
logical in view of their long preparation, enormous expenditure, nation-
al prestige, admiration of German technique, and racial temp rament.
The dominant military group would othervise forfeit its pover, and hence
will only be deterre! by surerier force. At present the nuws frem Tokyo
is of warlike propaganda and preparation in the midst cf hiphly emotional
excitement. This spasm of resentment and of reluctance to lose the al-
luring benefits of conquest would be natural whatever may bo their ulti-
mato decisicn.
2) A realization of the disastreus censeguences to them-
selves in prevoking Arerican ecororic und possible naval crrosition, with
the probatle ccoperation f Great Britain, und the int nsified Chinese re-

SAYS JAPAN GETS
ZINC NEEDED HERE
Expert Werns That Brass
Output is Crippled.
ROME, N.Y., Dec. 2. (A.P.) --- C. Donald Dallas, president of Revere
Copper & Bress Co., Ino., s aid today that shipments of zinc to Japan
are seriously delaying the manufacture of brass for the defense needs
of the United States and Great Britain.
"The same sine which we allowed to be deliwered to Japan," he as-
serted, "was so badly needed at the time by our brass manufacturers
that fabrication plants all over the country are being forced to curtail
produotion schedules when they should be inoreasing.
"This country shipped 3,775 tons of zinc to Japan in October, bringing
the total of 12,042 tons for the first ten months of 1940, every pound
of which was badly needed by domestic fabricators engaged in supplying
our own and British defense needs.
"You can't make cartridge brass without zinc. Brass is sued not only
for cartridges and shell cases, but also for the production of detona-
tion aps, rotating bands, fuse baps and many other parts in the arma-
ment and munition fields."
Mr. Dallas suggested either the findin of some new supply of refined
zine, or diverting zino which goes into t he production of die-cast decorative
automobile trim, radiator grilles and refrigerator hartmare to defense
needs.

It was my understanding after talking with you some

permis a withholding "in the interest of national defense" of pesential
war materials which might otherwise fall into the hands of our potential
amenies.
That an overwhelming majority of the Ameriean people
heartily approved the restrictions plsced upon exports of iron and steel
serap and that they would like to see the licensing system sxtended in
order to prevent the sale to Japen of other war materials is evident frem
the enclosed reproduction of a recent Gallup poll.
Many millions of Aaericans appreciate the very
limited character of embargos thus far imposed and recognise the short-
sightedness of a policy which permits to Japes continued acoess to besée
war materials the mupply of which strengthens Japan for further aggresalve
moves whieh may cut directly across the vital interests of our omm natiem.
They realise the dangers that are inherent if our policies in this matter
are to be directed not by broad considerations of public interest and
natiomal security, but rather by the mall nmber of business eoneerms
that stand to profit for a short time from & continuation of this
destructive trade. Partioularly since the tri-partite alliance of Germeny,
Italy and Japan do these Lmericens realise hew impertant is Chimese
0
resistance in order that Japan's capacity for further harm may be checked,
and the inconsistency therefore of allowing the national resourees of this
nation to be extensively utilised in a continued effort to breek domm
Chinese resistance and morale. They fail to understend the economie
ccnsiderations which appear to justify a depletion of our omn resourees
for the building of Japenese battleships and cruisers and the creetiem
thereby of addisiomal defense burdens fer the Amerioen people.

=== Page 511 === (confidence 0.99)
MR. HORNBECK
AIRS
January 22, 1941.
Fusel oil is a crude mixture of
amyl, butyl and propyl aloohols, but in
the trade the term fusel oil is
synonymous with amyl aloohol.
Fusel oil is a solvent used in the
manufacture of paints, laquers and air-
plane dopes. It may also be used in the
manufacture of smokeless powder. There
are many commercial molvents and although
fusel oil is not used extensively in the
manufacture of high explosives its use
for other purposes might release other
solvents for use in high explosives.
The impression to be gained from
the attached telegram seems to be that
the Japanese are possibly stocking up
on fusel oil or are purchasing for
shipment to Germany (the latter seems
to be the most likely possibility).
I do not see that there is anything
that the Department can do in regard to
this matter exoept transmit copies of a
paraphrase to ONI, MID, the Administrator
of Export Control, and the Commerce
Department.
FE:Jonds:FRE

THE SECRETARY OF STATE,
WASHINGTON.
394.24/
SIR:
I have the honor to attach to this despatch a
schedule of copper shipments to be made from this port
by the American-owned Chile Exploration Company during
the months of January and February 194l, which shows
that 12, 720 and 11, 350 long tons are bound for Japan
and the United States, respectively.
partment of State
MAERCIAL AFFAIRS
JAN 28 1941
DIVISION OF
The continued heavy shipments of copper to Japan
arouse a great deal of suspicion locally, because prior
to three or four months ago that country did not take
semi-refined copper from this port. It restricted its
purchases to low grade copper ores only.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
I have the honor to quote 1rom a report dated January 23,
194l, from the Commercial Attaché, as follows:
"Recent reports have been received in this office to the
effect that Alberto P. Rojas e Hijos, a well-known firm of customs
brokers in Mexico City, have contracted with a Japanese firm, whose
name is not known, for the delivery of 5,o00 tons of fluorspar ore
monthly at Acapulco, to be loaded in a Japanese steamer. This
ore, according to these reports, is to come from mines near Taxco,
Guerrero, and to be loaded in automobile trucks which the above
mentioned company is attempting to buy from the Gemeral Motors
Company. The Japanese purchaser of the Mexican ore, the reports
PS/VH
stated, would pay cash for the ore.
"Mr. William R. Hamer, Service Manager of the General Motors
of/
1214

=== Page 528 === (confidence 0.98)
IL
-2-
of Mexice, was accordingly consulted by this office with regard
to these reports, and he said that he had seen the contract re-
ferred to, cenfirming the details of it as given above. He added
that the Mexican firm had made inquiries of him for the purchase
of thirty automobile trucks but that his New York office, with which
he had cemmunicated by telephone, refused to consider the sale. He
added that according to the contract for the sale of the ore, which
he had seen, the Japanese steamship line, N.Y.K., was to furnish the
funds for paying for the ore.
"From another source, it is learned that there are two important
fluorspar mines near Taxco, Guerrero, from which a rich ore not re-
quiring smelting, may be had in large quantities. In the past, ore
from these mines has been transported by donkey-back some 21 kilometers
to Toluca, thence by rail to Tampico, for shipment by water to New
York. Hence, if Japanese purchasers are successful in consummating
the project outlined in the preceding paragraphs, it may be possible
for them to load the ore at Zacualpan, the site of t he mines, directly
into trucks.
"The trucks would then have to pass over a secondary road from
the mines to Taxce, a distance of several kilometers only, and from
Taxce to Acapulco would have available a paved road, a distance of
290 kilometers."
Respectfully yours,
Pierre de L. Boal
Chargé d'Affaires ad interim.

Dear Senator Bone,
I have been informed by the manager of the Seattle
Street Railway that there 1s no way of preventing many
tons of old copper trolley wire from the Seattle car lines
being shipped to Japan. It seems that while it cannot go
as sorap, it 1s to be sent to the Tacoma Smelter, melted
into ingots and sent in that form. It 1s common knowledge that
materials like this are constantly being sent to the Jananese
and it 1s hardly consistent with the president's proclamation
that the demooracies must stand together.
I aopreciate very much the great responsibility that
1s yours in these uncertain times and I am sure that you must
sense the feeling that a great majority of our people believe
that the worse thing that could happen would be to become
involved in a war. Any influence that you may have in divert-
ing this calamity will go to your lasting credit.
Very truly yours,
J. E. McRae, Manager

Dear Dr. Hornbeck:
During the 45 days from September 12th to October 26, 1940,
inclusive - a period, you will recall, when interest in the Far
Kastern situation was fairly high - we received from a clipping
service 939 editorials dealing with questions of American policies
in the Pacific. These came from 233 newspapers, published in 105
aifferent cities and towns, in 38 states. The states from which
we did not secure editorials during this period were Arizona, Idaho,
Kansas, Nevada, New Hampshire, New Mexico, North Dakota, South
Dakota, Tennessee and Vermont.
For the purpose of information, we have made a careful analysis
of these editorials in relation to certain major questions of
American policy. You may be interested in the results, and I shall
summarize them briefly.
(1) Attitude on Aid to China
PS/JI
60
favored aid, without advocating any special
method for giving such aid.
2
were opposed to giving any aid to China.
15
mentioned aid to China, but were editorially
non-committal.
75
advocated financial aid to China.
32
mentioned financial aid, but were editorially
non-committal.
11
favored the sending of munitions to China.
FILED
mentioned the sending of munitions, but were
editorially non-committal.

=== Page 537 === (confidence 0.99)
Stanley K. Hornbeck
-2-
January 2, 1941
(z) Upening of the Burma Road
25
favored American pressure or support for an
opening of the Burma Road.
3
were opposed to any such pressure.
29
mentioned the problem, but were editorially
non-committal.
(3) mbargoes on Trade with Japan
3
favored a general embargo on all trade with Japan.
3
mentioned such an embargo, without editorially
commitment.
233
advocated embargoes upon exports, in most cases
specifying war materials.
5
were opposed to such embargoes.
136
mentioned such embargoes, but were editorially
non-committal.
37
favored embargoes upon imports from Japan.
1
opposed any such embargoes.
41 mentioned import embargoes as a possibility,
without further editorial comment.
(4) Closer Ties with Australia. New Zealand,
The Dutch East Indies, etc.
40
favored closer relations.
24
opposed an extension of our relationships in
this area.
95
dealt with such relationships analytically,
without voicing editorial opinions.
(5) Additional U.S. Pacific Bases
16
favored the securing of additional bases.
32
mentioned the possibility, without editorial
comment.
(6) Improvement of Relations with Russia
32

were opposed to such efforts.

referred to Russian relations as significant,
but ventured no editorial opinions.

UNITED STATES SENATE

January 29, 1941.
Honorable Walter F. George, Chairman,
Committee on Foreign Relations,
U. S. Capitol Building,

Dear Senator George:
Yesterday I received a letter written on January 22
by my sister in Portland, Oregon, and I quote an exoerpt
from it. The explanations in parenthesis are mine:
"Hunt (Huntington Malarkey is my nephew and an
overseas veteran of the last World War, and today a
man of considerable financial importance) was at
our house tonight and was greatly disturbed and
anxlous that I should write you, as he had been
over to attend some business at the Columbia Air-
port today and saw 25 or 30 cars rolling by loaded
with rather queer-looking freight. On investigation,
it proved to be airplane beacons on steel towers--
hundreds of them--bound for Vladivostok, Russia--
thenoe to Japan! John Burgard (Mr. Burgard 1s chair-
man of the Portland Dock Commission and one of
Portland's most prominent citizens) took Hunt to
see other shipments to Russia--all equally alarming--
copper, steel, iron, etc."
I wish to make the following comments on this letter:
1 - will you kindly take steps necessary to verifying the
ahove facts, if you believe it of sufficient importance
or necessity?
2 - will you let me have your comments upon the circum-
stances, and whether you believe it a wise foreign policy
at the present time, for America to export such items of
merchandise to Russia?
3 - Although I sponsor the communication and authorize any
use of it which may serve a proper public function, I
request that no nublic reference be made to my s1ster,
as the author of it.
Very truly yours,
RUFUS C HOLMAN

SHOULD WE IMPOSE EMBARGOES ON JAPAN?
1241
UN OF
January 18, 1941
This paper discusses the question whether it would
be advisable at this juncture further to expand a program
of embargoes of commodities involved in our trade with
Japan.
The American public is committed to the view that
the supplying of war essentials to Great Britain 1s a
necessary step in defending the United States against Ger-
many. It 1s quite clear that if Germany were dependent on
394.24/1
the United States for war essentials a logical corollary
would be to withhold them from Great Britain's antagonist.
The Britieh would otherwise quickly point out that progress
toward a democratic victory would not be assisted if American
2
2
planes supplied to Britain had to be used up in destroying
2
the same number of planes supplied to Germany. In the
Atlantic, fortunately, we do not have to take measures to
2
withhold supplies from Germany, because Britain 1s still
strong enough to blockade Germany. The question of embargo
of Germany does arise in the Pacific, because Japan and the
A
c:
Soviet Union, Pacific powers, have both incurred obligation
to supply goods to Germany. The British have already
169
alleged that American oil, grain and cotton have either
been shipped through Vladivostok and delivered to Germany,
PC/MFM
or have served to release eguivalent amounts of oil, grain
and

=== Page 560 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
and cotton produced in Russia. The matter is presumably
under discussion with the Britieh. In the Pacific there
arises, also, the question whether the United States
should continue to trade with Japan. The British, al-
though threatened and oppressed by Japan, are not at war
with Japan and do not appear to have suggested that the
United States embargo that country; in fact, they appear
to be trading with Japan themselves. They appear to
have assented to the sale of 1,800,000 tons of petroleum
produots to Japan by American and British firms in the
NetherlandsEast Indies. If this is so there 1s some in-
consistency in their objecting to the sale of American
petroleum to Russia for transfer to Germany, for Japan,
also, is advantageously situated to ship supplies to Ger-
many, through Russia. Britain is at war in Europe; she is
afraid that Japan will attack British Far Eastern posses-
sions. The British attitude toward trade with Japan need
not necessarily influence the American attitude.
That the question of embargoes on trade with Japan
has an important bearing on the war in Europe arises from
the fact that Japan 1s hoping and working for the victory
of her German Axis partner. There have been Japanese
official announcements that Japan has formed with Germany
and Italy joint committees for economic, military and
political

=== Page 561 === (confidence 0.98)
-4-
statements Australia and New Zealand are not included in
the plan, but it is to be obeerved that the execution of
the plan would result in Japanese dominetion of the sea-
routes between the United States and those two dominions.
It is a particular objective of the United States to pre-
vent the eclusion of Amerioan shipping and enterprise
from this vast area. If Germany and Japan were to succeed
in their respective sohemes of imperialistic expansion
Britain would be blotted out as a powerful nation and the
United States would not only be relegated to the position
of a second rate power, but would be under continual threat
from the south, eest and west.
Without going back to the "Hay Doctrine" and the
Washington Conference Treaties, it may be said thet in
recent years the method most consistently adopted by the
United States to frustrate Japan's plan has been to assiet
China to obstruct Japan's first step, that is, the conquest
of China. Japen's attempt at this conquest has been pub-
licly condemned by the American Government in official
communioations and statements; at the inetance of the Ameri-
can Government there have been enforced "moral embargoes"
against the shipment of certain war materials to Japan;
the public has voluntarily diminished purchases of Japanese
goods; for purposes of rearming itself the United States
18

=== Page 562 === (confidence 0.98)
-5
is withholding from Japan materials important to armament;
the American Government has removed the treaty basis for
trade between Japan and the United States. The American
Government has strengthened China's defense by buying
Chinese silver, by granting credits up to $170,000,000,
and by public declarations; the public and the Government
have devoted considerable sums to civilian relief. The
Amerioan fleet has remained at the Hawalian Islands;
American oitizene have been advised and assisted to return
to the United States, both of which measures have been
interpreted by the public as meaning that the American
Government anticipates the outbreak of "trouble" in the
Far East. Because it is felt that Great Britain, France
and the Netherlands are not at present in position to
defend their interests in the Fer East against Japan, the
public has likewise inferred that these measures have
presaged trouble between the United States and Japan; that
is, that the American Government 1e making ready to take
stronger measures than hitherto in pursuance of its
up
opposition to the setting/of Japanese control over the
Fer East.
While the United States has assisted Chine, care hae
been taken not to confront Japen with only two alternetives,
those of conformity to Americen views or war with America.
We

=== Page 563 === (confidence 0.98)
-8-
We have continued to be friendly to Japan. The press
reports Congressman Hamilton Fish as asking Secretary of
War Stimson on January 16 whether the Navy 1s our first
line of defense, and the Secretary as replying, in part,
"Our first line of defense is our diplomacy, our efforts
to make and retain friends". Secretary of State Hull is
reported as saying to the House Foreign Affairs Committee
on January 15, 1n part, that,
"Notwithstending the course which Japen has
followed during recent years, this Government has
made repeated efforts to persuade the Japanese Gov-
ernment that her best interests lie in the develop-
ment of friendly relations with the United States and
with other countries which believe in orderly and
peaceful process among nations. We have at no time
made any threats."
In accordance with this friendly attitude we have continued
to grant Japan the usual privileges of trade and intercourse,
even in the absence of a treaty regulating such matters,
and have assured Japan that restrictions of certein exports,
of which she complained, were occasioned by our own needs
and were not applied against Japan.
The results of this dual policy may be summarized as
follows: our assistence to China has increased China's
ability to prolong the war of resietance, but such assist-
ance has greatly angered Japan. One muet reluctantly con-
clude that whether or not one goes so far as to regard
normal trade relations with Japan as "appeasement", a
policy

=== Page 564 === (confidence 0.98)
-7-
policy of friendly persuasion has accomplished and gives
hope of accomplishing nothing toward inducing Japan to
abandon plans for the creation of a "Greater East Asia".
It is possible that the desire to continue trade with
the United States has led Japan to refrain from further
steps that might be likely to provoke the United States
to declare war on Japan, such measures, for example, as
the seizure of the Netherlands Eest Indies, but it is
doubtful whether the further carrying out of the plan for
expansion is more than postponed. In the meantime the
delay 1s presumably not disadvantageous to Japan, since
it gives opportunity for the following things to occur:
(1) the winding up of the China "incident" without loss
of prestige, (2) the United States embroilment with Ger-
many, and (3) further preparations by Japen.
We have observed that neither economic essistance to
China nor the continuance of trade with Japen has aveiled
to induce Japan to abandon her attempt to conquer China
or to creete a "Greater East Asia" and that at the most
her desire to retain our trade has led merely to a defer-
ment of some of the further steps. Varioue related issues
bear on the question of whether increesed economic pressure
on Japan is desirable at this juncture.
It 1s

=== Page 565 === (confidence 0.99)
-8
It is asserted by some that the United States should
devote its attention exclusively to aiding Britain to
defeat Germany, because the defeat of Germany 1s a matter
of vital 1mportance to the United States and nothing
should be done that might lead to a declaration of war
or further hostile measures by Japan, since war with Japan
would diminish our ability to aid Britain. In reference
to a proposal to enforce export embargoes against Japan
it is urged:
(1) that if we do not peaceably continue to
supply petroleum products and other commodities to
Japan, Japan will declare war against us;
(2) or will seize the Netherlande East Indies;
(3) or will be incited to direct participation
in the hostilities on behalf of the Axis and will
attack Hong Kong or Singapore.
It seems very unlikely that Japan would declare war
against the United States, even were a total import embargo
as well as an export embargo declared ageinst her. The
gradual expansion of embargoes would be still less likely
to provoke such a drastic step. Japan is already engaged
in a war that has occupied her full resources for three
end e half years and has saddled her with a tremendous debt.
War against the United States would not, of itself, restore
to Japan the income end the materials she gains from trade
with the United States. On the contrary, it would be the
first effort of the United States, in the event of war with
Japan,

=== Page 566 === (confidence 0.99)
-9-
Japan, to out off all Japan's trade, except possibly with
Asia and Russia, and, in this connection, the mainland is
at present a source of impoverishment of Japan, rather
than of supplies. Russia would not be likely to come to the
aid of her old antagoniet. So Japan would have to count
on her stored supplies and hope for a speedy victory.
The ciroumatance that war with the United States would be
almost entirely naval would not mitigate the fact that it
would entail on Japan a huge additional monthly expense.
Whether the Government could manage to raise this addi-
tional sum muet be a matter of conjecture, but one must
suppose thet both the Government and the Japanese people
would be very reluctant to undertake the tesk. Altogether,
it seems highly probable that Japen would at least postpone
a declaration of war until the United States should be en-
gaged in wer with Germany, if that should occur, or at least
until the defeet of Great Britain by Germany, to the end
that Germany might afford her assistance.
The second possibility is that Japan might attempt to
compensate for the loss of American petroleum by trying to
seize the Netherlands East Indies, supported by the hope
that while enabling Japan to get petroleum the step would
not provoke war with the United States. There are two or
three reasons for thinking Japan would not undertake this
venture.

=== Page 567 === (confidence 0.99)
-10-
venture. In the first place, so far as Japan knows, the
United States might wage war to save the Netherlands East
Indies, since we also are dependent on some of their ex-
ports. The Secretary of State in 1940 issued three
declarations attesting to the seriousness with which the
Amerioan Government regards the maintenance of the status
quo in the Far East, and although Japan's incursion into
Indo-china has produced no American reaction of importance,
so far, Japan does not know at what point armed protest
will be made. In the second place, the capture of the
Netherlands East Indies is not likely to be an easy under-
taking. It is reported that the Dutch have land forces,
ships and planes; it is also reported that should this
resistance be overcome, and the Japanese troops seize the
oil fields, they would find the wells and all the physical
equipment destroyed, in which case it would take months to
restore them to production. The trein of dangers to which
Japan would be exposed 1s further lengthened by the chance
that if Japan were to find herself simultaneously engaged
in fighting China, the Netherlands East Indies and the
United States, Russia, so far from coming to her assist-
ance, would utilize this favoreble opportunity to redress
the wrongs of the war of 1905. It has been hazarded that
if the United States were to take warlike measures against
Japan, Japan might enter into an agreement with Russia to
divide

=== Page 568 === (confidence 0.99)
-11-
divide the Far East, receiving from Russia, in the mean-
time, such petroleum and other supplies as she might need
pending restoration of trading privileges. As already
obeerved,'it is hard to see why Russia should be willing
to enter into such an agreement at the very moment when
Japan's assistance would be of least value and when Russia
would be in the best position to avenge herself on Japan.
If an attempt by Japan to seize the Netherlands East
Indies is unlikely, an attempt to seize Singapore seems
still more so. It is quite possible that seizure of Hong
Kong would be allowed by Great Britein, as well es the
United States, to pass without armed protest, since the
possession of Hong Kong would not appreciably strengthen
Japan's position. An attack on Singapore, Jepan would
know, would be regerded by the United States, as well as
by Britain as a very serious event, and almost sure to
provoke the United States to war. The talk that hes al-
ready teken place, as reported in the press, concerning
possible joint use of Singapore as a naval base, muet have
warned Japan about this.
If one mey engage in a surmise, it may be assumed that
Germany's attitude toward the acquisition of the Netherlands
East Indies and Singapore by Japan is based on the supposition
that

=== Page 569 === (confidence 0.99)
-13-
Far East, with their attention engrossed by anticipation
of and involvement in hostilities in Europe. Circumstances
are still such that the United States alone, among the
great powers, is in position to influence strongly the out-
come of the Asiatic struggle. Nevertheless, if Japan
should succeed in subjugating China end then establish
military control of "Greater East Asia", Great Britain
would be greatly weakened in the fight against Germany.
Japan 1s an aotive pertner in the Axis. Support of China
and aid to Great Britain, as well as the protection of
American interests, ell make it desirable to weaken Japan
and impede its military activities.
China's determination and ability to resist the Japan-
ese invasion have exceeded the expectation even of those
who admire and respect the Chinese, but China entered the
contest under eevere handicaps end the issue 1s in doubt.
The lengthening of the period of hostilities may be
progreseively weakening Japan, but it cannot be ignored
that time is also a strain end a peril to China. Things
may happen. The seemingly endless disruption of liveli-
hood throughout huge masses of the population mey foster
desperation and "Communism"; bombs or fatal illnesses may
deprive China of some of her very important leaders. It
shal1

=== Page 570 === (confidence 0.99)
-14-
shall be altered in China's favor, so that China's ultimate
viotory shall not be in any doubt.
As the most immediately practioable means of further
assisting China it now seems desirable to proceed cautiously
to apply greater "economic pressure" on Japan, by placing
more exports on the list of embargoes and by beginning on
1mports. For reasons already set forth it does not seem
necessary to fear that such pressure, especially if it
were applied gradually, would cause Japen to enlarge her
already hopeless military commitments by declaring war on
the United States or by emberking on further attempts at
conquest. Japan's army would probably be very successful
ageinst any country less enormous in population and area
than Chine and Japan'e navy 1s admittedly large and sup-
posedly efficient. Nevertheless, Japan has the weakness
of a small, highly 1ndustrialized nation. This vulnerable
point the United States is able to attack and in defense
of the general interests, should attack, with caution, but
more bolaly than heretofore.

E. D. McMAHON
1709 Milam Building

January 18, 1941.
Mr. Henry Morgenthau,

Mr. Cordell Hull,
Secretary of State,
Washington, D. C.
Gentlemen:
SUGGESTED PLAN FOR KEEPING MEXICAN MERCURY, TUNGSTEN, MOLYBDENUM, AND
VANADIUM AWAY FROM JAPAN AND GERMANY.
We read in the New York Times, Business Week, and elsewhere, that a
lot of the above is going to Japan and Germany. We have been holding an "umbrella"
over Mexican finance for many years, through buying Mexican silver at a higher
price than Mexico otherwise would get. Why not tell Mexico "We will not buy your
silver BY ITSELF, but only in conjunction with mercury, molybdenum, etc., sold at
the same time. Illustrating, if Mexico could export 1,O00 units of silver, plus
one unit each of mercury, tungsten, molybdenum, and vanadium, we would pay a fixed
amount for same, delivered as a unit, but would not buy silver at any price, de-
livered by itself."
The one objection to the above, as I see it, would be that Nazi propa-
gandists would say that we were using pressure, that it was a hint as to what Argen-
tenia and other Latin countries would get later. Well, what of it? This "good
neighbor" policy is o.k., but the underlying principle should be AN IRON HAND BE-
NEATH A VELVET GLOVE. In this crisis there is no middle ground, the other countries
are either for us or against us, and that might as well be understood.
Again, "a loan oft loses self and friend". What we want is less loans,
and more buying of the ACTUAL CO!AODITIES, and storing them in this country as a
"hole card".
Sincerely,
(Signed) E. D. McMahon
EDMcM:e

=== Page 574 === (confidence 0.99)
osars. Morgenthau, Knudsen, & Hull,
Washington, D. C.
Jamuary 18, 1941,
Page - 2 -
P. S.
Perhaps the same system could be used with many other countries whose
prosperity is vitally dependent on their exports to the United States, such as
Brazil with its coffee, etc.

The Minister of Australia called at his request.
Confidential File
He handed me a statement (copy attached) concerning
an embargo imposed by his Government upon exports of
894.24
steel and scrap iron to Japan and countries other than
int
the British Empire for the purpose of conservation.
The Minister said he felt that the recent conferences
600.479
among naval officials had been of real value both to us
and to his country. There were, of course, no commitments
PS
of any kind either way, but tiere was a rather full ex-
/JI
change of information and ideas, both present and pros-
pective, about the situation.
LEB 4- 1941
The Minister then inquired whether we had been
giving

=== Page 579 === (confidence 0.98)
001064
-2-
giving further consideration to sending some naval ves-
sels to his country, and possibly to Singapore. I re-
plied that our first steps in dealing further with the
Japanese situation included a recent loan of 100,000,000
to China, the sending of a number of vessels, including
submarines and tenders, and some airplanes to the Philip-
pines. I added that there was a prospective embargo on
finished classes of iron and steel, on a quota basis,
with Great Britain and the Western Hemisphere excepted.
I concluded by saying that until these steps had been
completed, and we were then able to see and appraise the
situation, I could not undertake to o into nis inquiry.
The Minister seemed to understand and to acquiesce cheer-
fully.
C.H.

to other parts of the British Empire, as from 27th Novem-

During the financial year 1939-40 Australia exported
to Japan £A240,000 worth of iron and steel scrap.

=== Page 585 === (confidence 0.99)
HOUSTON COMMITTEE TO DEFEND AMERICA BY AIDING THE ALLIES
807-12 State National Bank Building
HOUSTON, TEXAS
January 30, 1941
Congressman Albert Thomae
New House Office Building
Washington, D. C.
My dear Albert:
Thanks a lot for your letter in connection with the
Lease-Lend Bill. I hope that you can vote the measure in
its final form.
Our Houston Committee to Defend America by Aiding the
Allies has instructed us to inform you that there are
large shipments of gasoline (approximately l00,000 barrels
each month going to Japan through Houston and other Texas
ports.) The shipment of this gasoline does not violate the
present orders of our government, for its octane is below
the prohibited standard. However, we have it on good
authority (a leading chemlst of Rice Institute) that the
Japanese can very easily chemically treat this gasoline so
that 1t readily can be coneumed by Japanese airplanes and
other military equipment in violation of America's program
of denying Japan war materials for its conquest of China.
I am sure that you approve America's policy of the open
door in China.
Because our port is withan your congressional dis-
trict, the Committee believes that you will be the proper
congressman to bring this matter to the attention of the
Department of State. I wonder if you will be good enough
to let us know what you will do in urging that the Depart-
ment of State take steps to stop this flow of petroleum
and its products including gasoline to Japan; and I hope
that you will see that these leaks are stopped at once.
For they are increasing the market values of these materials
1n our own country or making it difficult for our government
to prevent increases in prices and are feeding the war
machines that our nation 1s undertaking to help Britain
combat.

=== Page 586 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
combat. If Germany and Japan are supplied with enough of
these 'goods', it 1s very possible that they will be used
against American soldiers and sailors in the not distant
future.
Please let me know what success you have on this
matter in Washington.
Sincerely yours,
ROLLAND BRADLEY
Chairman
ROBERT J. PEDEN
Chairman, Subcommittee
on Communications

Your letter of December 14th, 1940, inclosing memorandum of
the same date with reference to certain telegrams to and from Mitsui
& Company of San Francisco is acknowledged.
The following report is submitted with reference to the above
referred to memorandum:
(a) With reference to the following quoted telegrams:
"Collect from New Orleans to Mitsui & Company 1l-22-40.
"KIRISIME MARU COMPLETED LOADING 12:1O PM TODAY 30,O0O DRUMS
CITEX GASOLINE. (Signed) E. W. Saybold and Co. R. Z. Williams."
This shipment was authorised by License No. EB 265 dated
9-13-40, covering 200,000 drums of uncracked gasoline, at
an estimated net cost of $1,412, 450.00; and products added
to gasoline at an estimated net cost of $591,000.00, furnished
by the City Service Oil Company to Japan. Present regulations
permit the shipment of gasoline which does not exceed 87 octane
or material from which by commercial distillation there cannot
be separated more than 3% of such gasoline, hydro-carbon or
hydro-carbon mixture. This shipment is, therefore, muthorised
under the present restrictions with reference to the exportation
of petroleum products.
With reference to the following quoted telegram, referred to
in your memorandum:
From St. Rose, La., to Mitsui & Company, 1l-22-40:
"ALMERIA LYKES DOCKED 2140 PM STARTED LOADING 3:30 PM
TODAY. (signed) E. H. Wellemeyer, Cities Service Oil."
This shipment was covered by License No. ED 1015 dated 10-9-40,
covering shipment of 3,510,000 gallons of lubricating oil at a
cost of $877,500.00 to Japan. The lubricating oil is of a
CONFIDENTIAL

the licensing of lubricating oil of 95 or more seconds
Saybolt Universal Viscosity at 2l00 F. with a viscosity index

authorised for shipment with an approved export license.

=== Page 598 === (confidence 0.99)
9984 Durant Dr.
Beverly Hills, Califormia
January 18, 1941
The Honorable Leland M. Ford
House of Representatives
Washington, D. C.
8ir:
Permit me to express my appreciation for your con-
sistent attitude toward rearmament and defense measures.
As one who has followed foreign affairs rather closely for
many years, I am deeply 1mpressed with the necessity for
action not only prompt but with continuity of purpose,
method and objective. As one of your constituents, may I
ask certain information of you?
While most of our attention has been centered in
Europe, certain phases of our relations with the Orient
have become confused. I refer particularly to our commerce
with Japan. While I appreciate the delicacy of Japan's
balanoe on the tightrope, and the care our State Department
must exercise to avoid any movement which might be construed
as upsetting that balance, thus giving Japan an exouse for
taking the plunge into the South Seas and the Indies, are
we not guilty of destroying the continuity of method in
our dealings with aggressor States in permitting such a
flood of war materials as we are now shipping to Japan?
While I understand that scrap iron, steel and pig iron
shipments have been curtailed, accurate figures with regard
to shipments not only of those materials but also of oil,
gasoline and cotton have not come to my notice. Certain
souroes maintain, without official denial so far as I know,
that our exports of oil to Japan have lately increased
enormously. Other sources claim the reverse. The matter
1s important and I therefore ask of you accurate information
as to the amount, peroentage of inorease or decrease monthly,
and value of shipments of such materials over the last few
years, and particularly over the period of the last few
months. If you are unable to give me that information,
perhaps you will inform me as to where I may obtain it.
I believe that a policy of appeasement toward any of
the present-day dictator states will prove stupid and ruinous,
and I seek to determine our own government's attitude toward
Japan.
Thanking you for your attention to my request, and with
best wishes, I remain,
Sincerely yours,
WILLIAM B. MURPHY

February 8th, 1941.
Department of State,
Washington, D.C.
Gentlemen:-
For some time past the management of the Timken Roller Bearing Com-
894.24/123
pany has been concerned with the question of sales to Russia and Japan.
At a recent meeting of the Board of Directors, I was delegated to
ascertain the Government's attitude toward such sales. We are, of course,
544.2y
aware that there are definite embargoes on certain products of a military
nature. Also, and more vaguely, there appears to be an attitude of dis-
approval of sales to these nations of some other products of less direct
military value.
Our bearing shirments to Russia and Japan have been a negligible
amount of our total production for the past few years but the latter
nation has been taking more of late, possibly as a result of diversion
of purchases from Germany and Sweden. Russian shipments have shown
little change.
At the present time the Japanese activity of our exporting agent has
practically ceased and the local man, who is English, has left the coun-
try. As a result, our inquiries now come from Japanese buyers in New
PS/
York. We have, however, a considerable volume of orders on the books.
We would appreciate receiving your advice as to the Government's
attitude toward continued acceptance of Japanese and Russian orders as
we have no desire to pursue policies contrary to the Government's under
present conditions.
Sincerely yours,
W Robert Jinken
W.Robert Timken.
Assistant to the President.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
Division of Controls
Memorandum
February 6, 1941.
"---- crude oil or any other material from which
by commercial distillation there can be separated more
than 3 per cent of aviation motor fuel, hydrocarbon, or
hydrocarbon mixture bo1ling 75° F. and 350° F. A.S.T.M.
Knock Test Method, which with the addition of tetra-
ethyl lead up to a total content of 3 c.c. per gallon
will exceed 87 octane number by A.S.T.M. Knock Test
Method. In addition, any crude oil or any other material
from which by commercial distillation there can be pro-
duced as distillate or residuum products having a viscosity
of 95 or more Saybolt Seconds Universal at 2100 F. with
a viscosity index of over 85."
Mr. G. S. Walden, Chairman of the Board of the
Standard Vacuum Company, called me on the telephone on
February 5 and suggested that if, in the last sentence
pertaining to lubricating oil, the term "commercial
distillation" were replaced by the term "commercial
processes", a greater number of petroleum products would
be included in the definition.

=== Page 608 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
Division of Controls
Memorandum
February 8, 1941.
I had a telephone conversation with Mr. Walden of the
Standard Vacuum Oil Company, in which we discussed the
following section of the suggested revision of the defini-
tion of petroleum products as written by Dr. P. M. Robinson:
"In addition, any crude oil or any other
material from which, by commercial distillation,
there can be produced, as distillate or residuum,
products having a viscosity of 95 or more seconds
Saybolt Universal at 2lo° F. with a viscosity index
of over 85."
Mr. Walden stated that this definition, as it reads
above, without any limitation as to the percentage of avi-
ation lubricating oil which can be produced and, therefore,
covering products which would only yield a fraction of one
per cent lubricating oil, would include practically all
the lubricating oils and he specifically stated that it
would include the product on which the Japanese asked him
he quote.
Mr. Walden said that he thought the definition quoted
above a very good one as it now reads, but that if it
included the t hree per cent clause it would materially
reduce the types of lubricating oils subject to the
licensing regulations.
Before I was able to tell Mr. Walden that the lubri-
cating oil definition referred to above had no percentage
limitation, he had suggested a definition which would
require

=== Page 609 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
require a license for all crude oils and petroleum pro-
ducts which would yield by commercial distillation more
than three per cent of a lubricating oil which would
have 50 seconds Saybolt Universal Viscosity at 2l00 F.
and a viscosity index of 50. However, he said that Dr.
Robinson's definition, as it now reads, would be just as
satisfactory.
In a conversation with Dr. Robinson on the same date,
I was informed that they did not intend to include any
percentage limitation and that unless they learned that
one was desirable, they would not incorporate a percentage
in the definition.
A. SCHNEE
Co:AS:JCP:LMW

Mr. David Houston of Hercules Powder Company called on
or State
me this morning in regard to sales of pine oil to Japan being
made by his company. Pine oil, of which the United States
1s the only producer, is used chiefly as a flotation reagent
2
for copper, zinc, and lead ores. Supplies in this country
have usually been ample, Mr. Houston said, but they are now
beginning to run short. Japan which normally purchased
about 70,000 gallons a year bought about 330,000 gallons in
1940 chiefly in the last quarter. One Japanese firm 1s now
asking for quotations on lo0 tons a month and another firm
on slightly smaller amounts. Mr. Houston said that this o1l
has other industrial uses but that the one he had mentioned
is the only one of which he knows which has a military
P
importance. Mr. Houston said that this oil is now being
/MFM
151941
in wooden barrels.
Mr. Houston

out that should pine oil exports be cut off the Japanese
would immediately increase their purchases of cresylic aoid.

THE AMERICAN HEFUBLICS
Dear Mr. Geist:
DEPARTMED OF STATE
With reference to your telephone conversation yesterday, Mr.
894.24/ 1242
Janssen of the Metals and Minerals Division has taken this
up with the Iron and Steel Scrap Institute.
There will undoubtedly be considerable interest in discarded
rails, but it is doubtful that the business is large enough
to justify a special trip. Hence, they would like to obtain
the following additional information regarding the rails:
Are the rails suitable for relaying, rerolling or are they
scrap rails? Are they street car rails or railroad rails?
Are they free from attachments -- switches, frogs and guards?
What is the approximate rate per yard of the rails? General
information as to location, such as, are they inland and if so,
how far from port?
Yours very truly,
Geurge Wythe,
Liaison Officer.

8

Metal and Ore Corporotion before and do not wish
to be a party to this class of transaction."

Minero is unwilling to be a party to a transaction of this sort
it will be most satisfactory if the result of this attitude is
that the shipment to Japan is prevented.
I will let you know wh't more I hear and parti-
cularly where the Cobalt was loeded and if possible where it ori-
ginated.
In the meanvhile, it only remeins to add thet
we have telegraphed to Santingo telling them that the Cobalt was

Export License was required.

STATE
Mr. A.S. Schnee
DIVISION OF
Divid on of Controls
State Department
Washington, D. C.
Dear Mr. Schnee:
I have tried unsuccessfully to contact you on the telephone for
31/2
the last week, to talk over the wording of the outline for petroleum
products for export control purposes.
If you will remember the original wording that I had covering
crude oil was " crude oil or any other material from which by
commercial distillation there can be produced". You suggested that
we should change this wording to "crude oil or any other material
from wnich by commercial processes there can be produced". I object-
ed to this wording and we decided that it might be well to amend this
to read "crude oil or any other materials from which by economically
feasible commercial processes there can be produced" and you request-
ed me to write you an interpretation of "economically feasible
processes".
I have discussed this matter at some length with Dr. Wilson and
he feels that we should recommend a return to "commercial distillation"
as the wording "economically feasible processes" could only be such
processes as are considered feasible in the United States today and an
interpretation of this would always be a personal interpretation. He
has suggested that instead of changing the wording that viscosity index
of the products at the bottom of the paragraph should be dropped to
65. By this change all Pennsylvania crudes and the Mid-Continent crudes
which are suitable for producing aviation lubricant would come under
export control while the Gulf Coastal and California crudes would be
allowed to be exported.
PS/M FM
One point I believe that we have missed is that the paragraph
we have been talking about applies only to rude oils and that you
have been fearful that steam refined stocks might be allowed to be
OCT 2 3 1941
exported which could easily be converted into aviation lubricants.
However, in the paragraph under aviation lubricating oils this point

=== Page 679 === (confidence 0.97)
Mr. A.S.Schnee
Page two
February 27, 1941
is well covered as it reads " Any lubricating oil of more than 95
Saybolt Universal Seconds viscosity at 2lo° F. with a viscosity
index of 85 or over." Steam refined stocks are used in many cases
as lubricating oils and technically this interpretation may be put
on the description as aviation lubricating oils.
Very truly yours
PreRobmison
P. M. Robinson
Assistant to Consultant
Petroleum Unit

=== Page 685 === (confidence 0.99)
TIMKEN
ROLLER BEARING
COMPANY
Q Q 卫 I
CANTON, ONIO
February 8th,1941.
Department of State,
Washington, D.C.
Gentlemen:-
For some time past the management of the Timken Roller Bearing Com-
pany has been concerned with the question of sales to Russia and Japan.
At a recent meeting of the Board of Directors, I was delegated to
ascertain the Government's attitude toward such sales. We are, of course,
aware that there are definite embargoes on certain products of a military
nature. Also, and more vaguely, there appears to be an attitude of dis-
approval of sales to these nations of some other products of less direet
military value.
Our bearing ahipments to Russia and Japan have been a negligiblo
amount of our total production for the past few years but the latter
nation has been taking more of late, possibly as a result of diversion
of purchases from Germany and Sweden. Russian shipments have shown
little change.
At the present time the Japanese activity of our exporting agent has
practically ceased and the local man, who is English, has left the coun-
try. As a result, our inquiries now come from Japanese buyers in New
York. We have, however, a considerable volume of orders on the books.
We would appreciate receiving your advice as to the Government's
attitude toward contimued acceptance of Japanese and Russian orders as
we have no desire to pursue policies contrary to the Government's under
present conditions.
Sincerely yours,
W.Robert Timken,
Assistant to the President.

of the Bank of the Manhattan Company, in regard to exports
of minerals and metals from Chile, Bolivia, Perú and
Argentina to Japan.
Certain of the statements as to sales of Bolivian tungston
by the Hoohsohild organization to Japan made by Dr. Hochsohild
in the fourth paragraph under the heading "Bolivia" of his
letter seem open to question. Dr. Hochschild states:
"We offered him (Mr. J. L. ROYDEN, blockade expert
of the British trade mission which visited La Paz last
month) to cease shipping to Japan by not buying wolframite
from our clients of the medium and small miners, but when
he found out that in that oase the wolframite would still
go to Japan, but instead of through us it would do so
through the Miners Bank, he suggested that we continue
these operations."
As reported in my despatch No. 581 of January 13, 1941,
Mr. Royden called at the Legation on that day and said that
one of the two blockade problems in Bolivia which were of
most concern to the British was that Germany was obviously
getting Bolivian tungsten and antimony through Japanese
dummies. In a later conversation, Mr. Royden told an officer
of the Legation that he had had no success in suggestions to
the Banco Minero that it cease shipping tungsten to Japan but
that he hoped that the Hochschild, Arameyo and Grace interests
would not sell to Japan as a result of conversations he had had
with them. This statement of Mr. Royden's would appear directly
contrary to Dr. Hochschild's assertion that Mr. Royden had
suggested that the Hochschild organization continue ts salos
to Japan. Without, of course, indicating the reason for the
Legation's inquiry, an officer of the British Legation who had
close contaot with Mr. Royden during his stay in Bolivia
was today asked whether it were true that Mr. Royden had mado
suot

=== Page 689 === (confidence 0.98)
- 3 -
such a suggestion. The answer was, "Poppycook".0.10 ε.w
Reference to enclosure No. 7 to my despatch No. 633 of
February 7, 194l, will show that shipments of Bolivian tungston
to Japan during the second semester of 1940 in which the
Hochschild interests figure. as shippers or consignees totaled
only 19 metric tons gross weight whereas such shipments by the
Banco Minero totaled 437 tons gross. If actual sales by the
Hoohsohild interests to Japan had in fact been only 19 tons
in these six months there would be no reason for bothering about
the matter at all.
. As has been previously reported, however,
it is asserted in tungsten trade circles that the general
Hochschild practice in the recent past, apparently in an
attempt to cover up the extent of the Hochschild sales finding
their way to Japan, has been to buy tungsten from small and
medium producers (Hochschild does not itself produce the
mineral in any appreciable quantities), then sell it to the
Banco Minero which in turn sells it to Japan from whence it
finds its way to Germany. That such transactions have been
taking place has been confirmed confidentially by an employee
of the Banco Minero. Consequently, the allegation by Dr.
Hochsohild that the tungsten bought by Hochschild and sold
by it direct to Japan would otherwise find its way to Japan
through purchase by the Banco Minero seems specious as most
of the Hochschild tungsten apparently passes through the
Banco Minero anyway.
The suggestions made on the last page of Dr. Hochscmld's
letter as to means of stopping South American shipments of ores
to Japan are for the most part completely valid so far as
Bolivian tungsten and antimony are concerned. Dr. Hochschild's
suggestion

=== Page 690 === (confidence 0.97)
-4-
suggestion that, because of the uncertainty of the Japanese
market, producers would be better off selling to the
United States under a contract for a term of years at a
fixed price even if it were lower than the price now
offered by the Japanese is pertinent. The same suggestion
as regards tungsten was made at the foot of page two of
my despatch No. 614 of January 30, 1941.
Respectfully yours,
For the Minister:
Allan Danson
Allan Dawson
Second Secretary of Legation.
